###ECONOMY###
ECONOMY AND ECONOMICS
● Economy is the state of a country or region in terms of the production and
consumption of goods and services and the supply of money.
●  Economics is the branch of knowledge concerned with the production,
consumption and transfer of wealth.
Various Types of Economy
● Capitalist Economy : An economic system based on private ownership of
capital and production inputs and on the production of goods and services for
profit.
Features of Capitalist Economy :
1. Private Property 2. Price Mechanism 3. Freedom of Enterprise 4.
Sovereignty of the consumers. 5. Profit motive 6. No government
interference 7. Democratic 8. Self Interests
● A Socialist Economy is characterised by social ownership and democratic
control of the means of production which may means autonomous
cooperatives or direct public ownership where its production is carried out
direct for use.
● A mixed economy is variously defined as an economic system consisting
of a mixture of either markets and economic planning, public ownership and
private ownership or free markets and economic interventionisms
● Indian economy is the example of this type of economy.
● Open Economy : It refers to a market economy which is generally free
from trade barriers and where exports and imports form a large percentage of
the GDP.
● Closed Economy : It is an economy in which no activity is conducted with
other economics.
The goal of economy is to provide consumers with everything that they need
from their own economy.
●  Economic Growth : An increase in the amount of goods and services
produced per head of the population over a period of time.
● Causes of economic Growth—
1. An increase in aggregate demand.
2. An increase in aggregate supply (productive capacity)



Sectors of Indian Economy—
●  Primary Sector : When the economic activity depends mainly on
exploitation of natural resources then that activity comes under the primary
sector. Ex-Agriculture and agriculture related activities.
● Secondary Sector : When the main activity involves manufacturing then it
is the secondary sector. All industrial production where physical goods are
produced come under the secondary sector.
● Tertiary Sector : When the activity involves providing intangible goods
like services then this is part of the tertiary sector.Ex - Financial services,
Banks, managements etc.
●  Organised Sector : The sector which carries out all activity through a
system and follows the law of the land is called organised sector.
● Unorganised Sector : The sector which evade most of the laws and don’t
follow the system comes under unorganised sector. Ex - tea stall, Grossery,
labour etc.
● Public Sector : Companies which are run and financed by the Government
comprises the public sector. Ex - BHEL, GAIL, SAIL, IOC etc.
● Private Sector : Companies which are run and financed by private people
comprises the private sector. Ex - Hero Honda, Tata, Maruti Suzuki etc.
General Principles of Economics
● Definition of Economics :
According to JB say : It is the science of production, distribution and
consumption of wealth.
According to Carl Menger : It is a science which studies human behaviour as
a relationship between ends and source means which have alternative usage.
Form of Economy :
1. Micro Economics 2. Macro Economics
(1) Micro Economics : It is the study of decisions of people and business
and the interaction of those decisions in markets.
●  The goal of microeconomics is to explain the prices and quantities of
individual goods and services. It also studies the effects of the Government
regulation and taxes on the prices and quantities of individual goods and
services.
(2) Macro Economics : It is the study of the national economy as well as
global economy and the way that a economic system work.



● The goal of macro economics is to explain general price level, national
income, employment, production. It also studies the effects of the
Government action-taxes, spending and the deficit on total incomes and price
level.
Monopoly : It is characterised by an absence of competition, which often
results in high prices and inferior goods. It is a market containing a single
firm.
Duopoly : A situation in which two companies own all or nearly all of the
market for a given product or services. It can have the same impact on the
market as a monopoly.
Oligopoly : It is a situation in which a particular market is controlled by a
small group of firms.
Perfect competition : It is the opposite of monopoly, in which only one firm
supplies a particular good or service, and the firm charge whatever it wants,
but under perfect competitions, there are many buyers and sellers, and prices
reflect supply and demand.
● Consumers have many substitutes.
● New firms can easily enter the market generating additional competition.
Monopolistic Competition : It is a market structure in which many firm sell
products that are similar but not identical.
● Characteristics—
(i) Many sellers (ii) Product differentiation (iii) Free ending.
Engel’s Law : The law which says that people generally spend a smaller part
of their budget on food as their income rises. The idea was suggested by
Ernst Engel, a Russian Statistician in 1857.
Laffer Curve : The Laffer curve is one possible representation of the
relationship between rates of taxation and the hypothetical resulting levels of
government revenue. Tax Revenue O 35% 70% Tax Rate
Okun’s Law : The relationship between an economy’s unemployment rate
and its gross national product (GNP)
● 20th century economist Arthur Okun developed this idea.
Greshm’s Law : When a government overvalues one type of money and
undervalues another, the undervalued money will leave the country or
disappear from circulation into hoards, while the overvalued money will
flood into circulations. It is commonly stated as ‘Bad Money drives out good
money.’



Phillip’s Curve : The inverse relationship between rate of inflation and rate
of unemployment is shown in the Phillip’s curve, pirce stability has a trade-
off against employment.
Some level of inflation could be considered desirable in order to minimize
unemployment.
Kuznets Curve : A kuznets curve graphs the hypothesis that as an economy
develops, market forces first increase and then decrease economic inequality.
Inequality
Income per capital
Giffen Good : When the price of an item increases, its demand should
decrease, but there can be a situation when even after Price rise, the demand
increases such goods are called Giffen goods. They violate the general law of
supply and demand. This concept was first identified by a statistician, Robert
Giffen that is why these called Giffen goods.
Inflation : Inflation is the rate at which the general level of Prices for goods
and services is rising and, consequently, the purchasing power of currency is
falling.
Types of Inflations
(1) Demand Pull Inflation : This types of inflation occurs when demand
exceeds supply.
(2) Cost - Push Inflation : This happens when there is an increase in the
cost of raw materials needed to produce the goods. Ex– Fuel costs. when the
cost of transport goods goes up, Firms have higher output costs.
This causes less goods being offered and results in higher prices for those
goods that are available.
Deflation : Deflation occurs when the general prices of goods and services
of an economy falls for a significant period of time. It is directly related to the
supply and demand of money. It occurs when the supply of money increases
slower than the supply of goods and services we purchase with it.
Stagflation : It is an economic cycle in which there is a high rate of both
inflation and unemployment.
It occurs when the production of goods and services in an economy slows
down or even starts to decline. It is a period of no growth or even an
economic contraction of an economy. It causes a high degree of
unemployment because we don’t need employees of we are not producing.



Depression : A depression is any economic downturn where real GDP
declines by more than 10% for a long period. In depression an extremely low
aggregate demand in the economy causes activities to decelerate.
Recession : It is a period of general economic decline and is typically
accompanied by a drop in the stock market, an increase in unemployment,
and a decline in the housing market.
In general a recession is less severe than a depression.
Factors of Recession
(1) High interest rate (2) Inflation (3) Reduced consumer confidence (4)
Reduced real wages.
Deflation : In economics, deflation is a decrease in the general level of
goods and services. It occurs when the inflation rate falls below 0%
Disinflation : It is a decrease in the rate of inflations, a slow down in the rate
of increase of the general price level of goods and services in a nation’s gross
domestic product over trip. It is the opposite of reflation.
Reflation : It is the act of stimulating the economy by increasing the money
supply or by reducing taxes, seaking to bring the economy (Specifically price
level) back up to the long-terms trend, following a dip in the business cycle.
Consumer Price Index (CPI) : A consumer price index measures changes
in the price level of a market basket of consumer goods and services
purchased by household. The annual percentage change in CPI is used as a
measure of inflation.
Wholesale Price Index (WPI) : A wholesale price Index is the price of a
representative basket of wholesale goods. The WPI focuses on the price of
goods traded between corporations, rather than goods brought by consumers,
which is measured by CPI.
Index of Industrial Production (IIP) : The Index of Industrial Production is
an index for India which details out the growth of various sectors in an
economy such as mining, electricity and manufacturing. The all India IIP is a
composite indicator that measures the short-term changes in the volume of
production of a basket of industrial products during a given period with
respect to that in a chosen base period.

SOCIAL AND ECONOMIC DEVELOPMENT INDEX
● Human Development Index (HDI) : The UN Human Development Index
is a standard means of measuring well-being, index was developed in 1990



by the Pakistani economist Mahbub-ul-Haq and has been used since 1993 by
the United Nations Development Programme in its annual report.
Indicators of HDI :
●  A long and healthy life, as measured by life expectancy at birth.
Knowledge, as measured by the adult literacy rate and the combined gross
enrolment ratio. A decent standard of living as measured by gross domestic
product (GDP) per capita at purchasing power parity (PPP) in US Dollars.
IHDI (Inequality -adjusted Human Development Index) :
●  IHDI adjusts the HDI for inequality in distribution of each dimension
across the population.
INDIA : A MIXED ECONOMY
● The Indian economy is a mixed economy.It has acquired this form with the
growth of a large public sector since Independence.
GDP : It is the official measure of the total output of goods and services in
the economy. It is the total market value of all final goods and services
produced during a given time period within a nation’s domestic border.
● The word ‘domestic’ in GDP means that we are only counting things that
are produced within our domestic border whether they are produced by
foreigners.
GDP = C + I + G + (X – M)
C = Consumption I = Investment G = Government Spending X = Export M =
Import
Gross National Product (GNP) :
GNP is the market value of all the products and services produced in a year
by the citizens of a country.
● This includes any income earned by residents from overseas investments
minus income earned within the domestic economy by overseas residents.
GNP = GDP + (X –M)
X = Earned income in foreigns by citizens
M = Earned income by foreigners in a country When X = M, GDP would be
equal to GNP, this type of economy will be closed economy.
Net Domestic Product (NDP) :
Net Domestic product equals the gross domestic product (GDP) minus
depreciation. Net Domestic product accounts for capital that has been
consumed over the year in the form of housing, vehicle, or machinery
deterioration.



NDP = GDP – depreciation
Net National Product (NNP)
When Charges for depreciation are deducted from the gross National product
(GNP), we get net national Product (NNP).
NNP = GNP – depreciation
National Income : It is also known as National Income at factor cost. NNP =
National IncomeFC
NI = NNP + Subsides-Indirect taxes
Disposable Income (DI) : The Income left after the payment of direct taxes
from Personal Income is called disposable income. DI = PI – Direct Taxes
Per capita Income : Per capita income of a country is derived by dividing
the national income of the country by the total populations of a country. PCI
= Total National Income Total National Population
National Income : Important Concepts
1. GNPMP = GDPMP + Income from Abroad
2. NNPMP = GNPMP – Depreciation
3. NDPMP = GDPMP – Depreciation
4. GDPMP = GNPMP – Income from Abroad
5. NNPMC = GNPFC – Depreciation
6. GNPFC = GNPMP – Indirect Taxes + Subsidy
7. GDPFC = GDPMP –Indirect Taxes + Subsidy
8. NNPFC = GNPMP –Depreciation– Indirect Taxes + Subsidy
9. NDPFC = GDPMP –Depreciation– Indirect Taxes + Subsidy MP =
Market Price FC = Factor Cost GNP = GNI (Gross National Income) NNP =
NNI (Net National Income)



● Public Account : This is an account in which public money is put in and
Government acts only as banker. It is operated by executive Action.
There is no need to take consent of parliament to withdraw money from this
account.
● Contigency Fund : This is the fund which is kept at disposal of president
to meet unforeseen expenditure. It is operated by secretary, ministry of
finance on behalf of the president.
● Public Receipts : Every receiving to a government by revenue and non
revenue sources is a receipt. It includes all income as well non incomed
accruals of government. It is divided into parts— (1) Revenue Receipts (2)
Capital Receipts.
●  Revenue Receipts : These receipts do not create any corresponding
liability for the government. These receipts don’t cause any reduction in
assets of the government.
Revenue receipts of a government are of two kinds :
(1) Tax revenue (2) Non-Tax revenue
(1) Tax Revenue : This includes all money earned by the government via
different taxes the government collects.
(2) Non-Tax Revenue : This includes all money earned by the government
from sources other than taxes. Ex– Fees, Fines, Escheat, Special Assessment,
Income from Public Enterprises, Grants.
● Capital Receipts : These receipts create a liability from the government.
These receipts cause reduction in assets of the government.
Capital receipts are classified as given below—
●  Recovery of Loans : These are capital receipts because they reduce
financial Liability of the government.



●  Borrowings : Funds raised by the government from the borrowing are
treated as capital receipts such receipts create liability.
● Other Receipts : Funds raised through disinvestment are included in this
category. By this government assets are reduced.
Comparison Chart
Basis for Capital Receipts Revenue Receipts

Basis for Capital Receipts Revenue Receipts
Comparison

1. Meaning Capital receipts are the Income Revenue receipts are the
generated from Investment and income generated from
financing activities of the the operating activities of
business the business

2. Term Long term Short term
3. Showing Balance Sheet Income Statement
4. Nature Non recurring Recurring
 
● Revenue Expenditure : An expenditure which do not creates assets or
reduces liability is called revenue Expenditure Ex– Salaries of government
employees, interest payment on loan taken by the government, pension,
subsidies, grants, etc.
● Capital Expenditure : It refers to the expenditure which leads to creations
of assets and reduction in liabilities. Ex– Constructions works, roads,
bridges, etc.
● Planned Expenditure : Any expenditure that is incurred on programmes
which are detailed under the current plan of the centre or centre’s advances to
state for their plan expenditure. Provisions of such expenditure in the budget
is called planned expenditure. Ex–Expenditure on electricity generations
irrigation and rural developments. Construction of roads, bridges, canals,
science & Tech, pollution control etc.
●  Non Planned Expenditure : This refers to the estimated expenditure
provided in the budget for spending during the year on routine functioning of
the government. It is all expenditure other than balanced expenditure of the
government. Ex–Expenditure on police, Judiciary, military etc.
INDIAN TAX STRUCTURE



Tax is a compulsory payment by the citizens to the government to meet the
public expenditure. Tax can be direct or indirect.

Direct Taxes : The taxes that charged upon and collected directly from the
person or organisation that ultimately pays the tax.
List of Direct Taxes :
(i) Corporate Tax : It is levied on the profit of the companies or corporations.
— It is the largest source of revenue of the Central Government.
(ii) Income Tax : It is the tax levied directly on the income of the people by
the Central Government.
(iii) Gift Tax : This tax is imposed by the Central Government on all
donation and gifts over and above the prescribed limits to the family
members.
(iv) Wealth Tax : This tax is lived on the net wealth of the individuals, Hindu
undivided family and joint stock companies. It is removed from Indian tax
system since April 2015.
Indirect Taxes : The tax which has incidence and impact at the different
points is the indirect tax.
As, for example, excise, sales tax, etc, which are imposed on either the
producers or the traders, but it is the general consumers who bear the burden
of tax.
List of Indirect Taxes :
(i) Custom Duties : These duties are imposed on commodities, which are to
be imported or exported from India.
(ii) Central Excise Duties : These duties are imposed by the Central
Government on the goods produced within the country except certain goods,
on which State Government are empowered to impose tax.
These goods include liquor drugs, etc.
(iii) Service Tax : It is a tax imposed on the person, who avails any specified
service.
— This tax was introduced in India in 1994-1995.



— With Economic growth and expansion of service sector in the economy,
revenue from service tax has been increasing over the years.
(iv) Value Added Tax (VAT) : VAT is a method of tax collection as well as
name of a state level tax in India.
— A tax collected at every stage of value addition, ie either by production or
distribution is known as value added tax.
— Value added refers to the difference between value of output and value of
intermediate consumption.
(v) Central Value Added Tax (CENVAT) : In the year 1996, the central
government started collecting its excise duty on the VAT method and the tax
was given a new name CENVAT.
Goods and Service Tax (GST) : The GST will simplify and harmonise the
indirect tax regime in the country — It will be imposed all over the country
with the uniformity of rate and will replace multiple central and state taxes.
— The GST will be levied both on goods and services.
— It would have a dual structure a central component levied and collected by
the centre and a state component administered by state.
Benefits of GST :
— GSt will simplify the indirect tax regime with a single tax.
— It will boost the GDP growth of country.
— GST will mobilise revenue systematically and reduce the fiscal deficit. —
Removal of cascading taxes makes the manufactring sector more competitive
and cut down the tax compliance burden.
Tax related terminology :
Tax Avoidance : It is the legal usage of tax regime to one’s own advantage,
to reduce the amount of tax that is payable by means that are within the law.
Tax Evasion : It is the illegal evasion of taxes by individuals, corporation
and trusts.
Tax Heavens : A tax heaven is a country or territory where certain taxes are
levied at a low rate or not at all individuals or corporate entities can find it
attractive to move themselves to areas with reduced or nil taxation levels.
Tax Buoyancy : It refers to the percentage change with the growth of
national income. That is growth based increase in tax collections.
Tax Elasticity : It is defined as the percentage change in tax revenue in
response to the change in tax rate and the extension of coverage. Buoyancy
on the other hand is the response to economic growth when the base
increases but there is no change in the rate.



Infrastructural facilities : Often referred to as economic and social
overheads, consist of :
(a) Energy : coal, electricity, oil and non-conventional sources.
(b) Transport : Railways, roads, shipping and civil aviation.
(c) Communications : Posts and telegraph, telephones, telecommunications,
etc.
(d) Banking, finance and insurance.
(e) Science and technology.
(f) Social overheads : health and hygiene and education.
LIST OF MAHARATNA, NAVRATNA AND MINIRATNA
Maharatnas :
Maharatna scheme was introduced for central public sector Enterprises
(CPSEs), with effect from May, 2010 in order to empower mega CPSEs to
expand their operations and emerge as global giants.
To be eligible for the grant of Maharatna status, the company should have an
average turnover of over Rs. 25,000 crore, average annual net worth of more
than Rs. 15,000 crore and average annual net profit over 5,000 crore during
the last three years. Besides, it should be a shareholding under the SEBI
regulations and have global presence.
Maharatna (Total no. of Maharatna is seven)
1. Indian Oil Corporation Limited (IOC)
2. NTPC Limited
3. Oil & Natural Gas Corporation Limited (ONGC)
4. Steel Authority of India Limited (SAIL)
5. Coal India Limited (CIL)
6. Bharat Heavy Electricals Limited (BHEL)
7. Gas Authority of India Limited (GAIL)
Navratnas :
Government introduced the Navaratna PSEs in 1997. Initially there was 9
Navaratna companies, many more central public sector Enterprises added in
Navratna list. Today there are 17 CPSEs in Navaratna companies list.
Medium Enterprises :
Manufacturing : Investment in plant Sector and machinery more than Rs. 5
crore, but does not exceed Rs. 10 crore.
Service Sector : Investment in equipments more than Rs. 2 crore, but does
not exceed Rs. 5 crore.



History of Planning in India
1934 Vishvesvaraya Plan in his book ‘The planned economy in India’ 1938
Nehru’s Congress Plan but not implemented.
1944 Bombay Plan by Industrialists such as JRD Tata, P. Thakurdas, GD
Birla, K. Lalbhai.
1944 The Gandhian Plan by Narayan Agarwal, given importance to
agriculture and cottage industries.
1945 M.N. Roy’s People’s Plan – with socialist learning.
1950 Jayprakash Narayan’s Sarvodya Plan based on Vinoba Bhave’s
Philosophy.
1952 NDC made by cabinet resolution.
2014 Modi shutdown Planning Commission.
2015 Government notified the formation of Niti Ayog.
NITI AYOG
● Niti Ayog or National Institution for Transforming India Ayog is a policy
think tank of government that replaces Planning Commission and it aims to
involve the states in economic policy making in india. It will be providing
strategic and technical advice to the central and the state governments.
● Prime Minister of India leads the Ayog and its chairperson.
● Government announced formation of Niti Ayog on 1st January, 2015.
● The Niti Ayog comprises of the following— (i) Prime Minister of India as
the Chairperson
(ii) Governing council comprising the chief ministers of all the states and
lieutenant-Governer of UTs.
(iii) Regional councils will be formed to address specific issues and
contingencies impacting more than one state or a regions. These will be
formed for a specified nature. The regional council will be convened by the
P.M. and will comprise of the CMs of states and Lt. Governers of UTs.
These will be chaired by the chairperson of the NITI Ayog or his nominee.
(iv) Vice-chairperson will be appointed by the PM. CEO – To be appointed
by the PM for a fix tenure.

POVERTY AND THE PLANNING PROCESS IN INDIA
The Concept of Poverty
● Poverty can be defined as a social phenomenon in which section of the
society is unable to fulfil even its basic necessities of life.
Lakdawala Committee
● Committee formed in September 1989 for estimating total no. of poor by
Planning Commission under the chairmanship of D.T. Lakdawala.
Tendulkar Committee



● Planning Commission formed the committee under the chairmanship of
Tendulkar, report submitted in December 2009.
●  This committee gave importance to expenditure on health, education,
clothing in addition to food in its definition of poverty.
SR Hashim Committee
●  The planning commission has constituted an Expert group under the
chairmanship of SR Hashim on May 2010.
●  The panel has suggested that the government should use threestage
approach-automatic exclusion, automatic inclusion, and scoring index to
identify urban poor.
Rangarajan Report on Poverty
● Planning commission constituted committee under the chairmanship of Dr.
C. Rangarajan in June 2012, to review the methodology for measurement of
poverty in the country.
The percentage of people below the poverty line in 2011- 12 was 30.95% in
rural areas and 26.4% in urban areas.
TYPES OF POVERTY
1. Absolute Poverty :
● When the level of the income of people of a country is so low that they
cannot meet even their basic minimum requirements, it is called absolute
poverty.
2. Relative Poverty
●  When we compare the incomes of different people we find that some
people poor than others, it is called relative poverty.
Types of Unemployment
● Open unemployment : The labourers do not find a work to do.
Unskilled labour unemployment is included in this category of
unemployment.
● Structural Unemployment : This is related to economic structure of the
country. It is found when the demand for labour is unable to keep pace with
the supply, due to high growth rate of population and low rate of capital
formation. It is of long-term nature and India’s unemployment is mainly of
this type.
● Under employment : The person is engaged in work but his efficiency
and capability is not being utilised to the optimum level.



●  Disguised unemployment : If a person is engaged in a work but his
contribution of marginal productivity is zero then it is called disguised
unemployment.
This is mainly found in agriculture sector of developing economies.
Removing these disguised unemployed from work will not affect total
production.
● Seasonal unemployment : This arises because demand for labour changes
with seasons and so workers get employment only in the peak season like
harvesting and sowing seasons in agriculture.
● Frictional unemployment : The unemployment results due to changes in
demand and supply conditions in the market. It results when workers are
shifting over from one job to another.
● Voluntary unemployment :
There are jobs and people are not ready to take job at the prevailing wage
rate. This generally exists in advanced countries at large.

AGRICULTURE
Agriculture is a very important sector of the Indian economy. It plays a major
role in the overall development of the country as it contributes nearly one-
fifth of GDP and engages around 60 per cent of the population of the
country.
LAND REFORMS
● At the time of Independence, there were three types of land tenure systems
prevailing in the country viz., the Zamindari system, the Mahalwari system
and the Ryotwari system.
● The period from First Five Year Plan to Third Five Year Plan concentrated
on land reforms and institutional changes.
●  The steps took include abolition of Zamindari and tenancy reforms for
bringing about rapid agricultural growth.
● The aims and objectives associated with land reforms include :
i. Abolition of intermediaries ii. Security of rent iii. Ownership rights for
tenants iv. Redistribution of land v. Consolidation of holdings vi. Cooperative
farming,
GREEN REVOLUTION
It refers to the major increase in agricultural productivity due to the
introduction of High Yield Variety of Seeds (HYVS) together with new
techniques.
● Green Revolution led to a significant increase in the production of both
Wheat and Rice.
● The credit for ushering in the era of Green Revolution goes to Dr. Norman
Borlaug and Dr. M.S. Swaminathan.
● Dr. William Goud gave the term Green Revolution.
●  Green Revolution initiated the process of modernisation of Indian
Agriculture.
● Its negative aspect was that Green Revolution remained confined to wheat
and rice only. It did not cover pulses.

AGRICULTURE PRICE POLICY
● The Commission on Agricultural Costs and Prices (CACP) provide expert
advice to the government on price policy.
●  The Food Corporation of India (FCI) has been the Government’s
operational arm for entering the market to guarantee price support to farmers.
● Minimum Support Price (MSP) :
MSP is the price which is announced by the Government to purchase the
crop from the farmers directly if they are willing if crop prices become lower
than MSP.
● CACP recomends Minimum Supprt Price (MSPs) of 23 commodityies.
● Which comprises 7 cereals, 5 pulses, 7 oilseeds and 4 commercial crops, to
the central government.
● The Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs (CCEA) gives approval for
the MSPs.
● For Sugarcane instead of MSP, “Fair and Remunerative Price” is declared.



● The main objectives behind MSPs are :
● To prevent fall in price & To protect the interest of farmers.
● Procurement Prices : Procurement prices are fixed at a higher levels as
compared to the MSP and are meant essentially for the purchase of quantities
needed by the Government for maintaining the PDS and for building the
buffer stocks.
●  Issue Prices : It indicates the prices at which the Government supplies
food grains through fair price shops and national depots.
NATIONAL BANK FOR AGRICULTURE AND RURAL
DEVELOPMENT (NABARD)
● NABARD was established on July 12, 1982. It was established with a paid
up capital of Rs. 100 crore having 50:50 contribution of Indian Government
and RBI.
●  NABARD is the apex institution providing finance for agriculture and
rural development.
Operation Flood
●  The largest ever dairy development project was launched in 1970,
popularly known as Operation Flood, with the object of setting up AMUL
like organisation in several states linking rural milk procurement points with
urban demand centres.
●  AMUL (Anand Milk Union Ltd.) at Anand in Kheda district of Gujarat
was set up in 1960s to promote cooperative movement in milk production
and marketing. AMUL subsequently began to serve as a model for future
development of dairy industry.
● Operation Flood II, launched in 1979, aimed at setting up a National Milk
Grid by linking rural milk sheds to major demand centres in urban areas.
● Now, India ranks first in the world in milk production.
MONEY & BANKING
Supply of Money
● There are four types of currency published in India called –M1, M2, M3,
M4.
● RBI calculates the four concepts of money supply in India
● M1 is called the narrow money



●  Currently Narrow Money (M1) and Broad money (M3) are relevant
indicators of money supply in India.
M0 = Reserve money or high power money currency with the public + other
deposits with the RBI + CRR themselves + CRR held with RBI.
M1 = Currecncy with the public + Demand deposit with the banking system
+ other deposit with the RBI.
M2 = M1 + saving deposits of post office, saving banks.
M3 = M1 + Time deposit with the banking system.
M4 = M3 + All deposits with the post office saving (excluding NSC)
Supply of Money M1

Currency with the public Demand Deposits with the Banks Other Deposits
with Reserve Bank Demand Deposits with Reserve Bank of Public Financial
Institutions Demand Deposits with Reserve Bank of Foreign Central bank
and Governments Demand Deposits of International Financial Institution
● Coins in India are limited legal tender.
● Paper currency is unlimited legal tender.
●  Narrow money is the most liquid part of the money supply because the
demand deposits can be withdrawn anytime.
Forms of Money
1. Fiat Money : Fiat money is money whose value is not derived from any
intrinsic value or guarantee that it can be converted into a valuable
commodity (Such as gold) : instead, it has value only by government order.
2. Credit Money : Credit money is money that is backed by a promise to
pay made by someone other than the state.
3. Fiduciary Money : Money that depends for its value and confidence that
is an accepted medium of exchange. It originated as a paper certificate that
was a promise to pay a certain amount of gold or silver to the bearer.
1. Deficit : Budget deficit refers to a situation when budget expenditures of
the government greater than the budget receipts.
There are three types of budget deficit—
1. Revenue Deficit 2. Fiscal Deficit 3. Primary Deficit
●  Revenue Deficit : It is excess of total revenue expenditure of the
government over its total revenue receipts. It is related to only revenue
expenditure and revenue receipts of the government.



● Revenue deficit signifies that government’s own earning is insufficient to
meet normal functioning of government departments and provision of
services.
● Revenue deficit results in borrowing. It occurs when government spends
more than what it collects by way of revenue, it incurs revenue deficit.
Main Remedies to Control
1. Government should raise rate of taxes. 2. Any new taxes
3. Reduce expenditure. 4. Avoid unnecessary expenditure
2. Fiscal Deficit : It is defined as excess of total budget expenditure over
total budget receipts excluding borrowing during a fiscal year.
●  Fiscal Deficit = Budget Expenditure (revenue + capital) — Budget
Receipts other than borrowings (revenue receipts + capital receipts other than
borrowings).
3. Primary Deficit : The borrowing requirements of the government include
interest obligations on accumulated debt. The primary deficit indicates the
deficit amount that the government needs to borrow for consumption and
investment purposes.
Primary Deficit = Gross Fiscal Deficit - Net Interest Liabilities
Objectives of Government Budget :
1. Economic Growth 2. Reduction of Poverty
3. Reduction of inequalities 4. Redistribution of incoming
5. Reallocation of resources 6. Price Stability
7. Financing and management of public enterprises.
Types of Budget : Budgets are of three types— (1) Balanced Budget (2)
Surplus Budget (3) Deficit Budget
1. Balanced Budget : A government budget is said to be a balanced budget
in which government estimated receipts are equal to government estimated
expenditure.
Government Receipts = Government expenditure It ensures financial stability.
It avoids wasteful expenditure.
2. Surplus Budget : When government receipts are more than government
expenditure in the budget the budget is called a surplus budget.
Estimated Govt. Receipts > Estimated Govt. Expenditure
● A surplus budget shows that government is taking
away more money than what it is pumping in the economic systems.
3. Deficit Budget : When government estimated expenditure exceeds
government receipts in the budget, the budget is said to be a deficit budget.



Deficit Budget = Govt. Expenditure > Govt. Receipt
Overall Balance of payments = Balance of payments on current account +
Balance of payments on capital account.
BALANCE OF PAYMENT
Balance of Payments on Current Account–It is made up of exports and
imports expenses on travel, transporation, insurance, investment, income,
transfer payments. It consists of visibles and invisibles.
Visibles refer to commodity exports and imports because these are recorded
at the custom barriers of the country.
Invisibles refer to service exports and imports e.g., transport charges,
shipping freight, passenger fares, harbour and canal dues, commercial
services (fees and commissions), financial services (brokers, fees), services
connected with the tourist traffic, payment of interest on external debt, and
inward remittances of funds by Non-resident Indians.
Balance of Payments on Capital
Account—It is made up of capital transactions e.g., borrowing and lending
of capital, repayment of capital, sale and purchase of securities and other
assets from foreigners—individuals and Governments.
It is the overall balance of payment which must be favourable.
Balance of Payments and Balance of Trade
Balance of trade = Value of commodity exports—Value of commodity
imports i.e. balance on visibles alone.
Balance of payments refers to the sum of both visibles and invisibles.
Balance of payments = Trade balance + Net invisibles
Foreign Direct Investment (FDI)
● FDI is a type of investment thai involves the injection of foreign funds into
an enterprises that operates in a different country of origin from the invester.
●  FDI play an extraordinary and growing role in global business. It can
provide a firm with new markets and marketing channels, cheaper production
facilities, access to new technology, products, skill and financing.
● FDI occurs when a company invests in a business that is located in another
country and it is investing not less than 10% of shares belonging to the
foreign company. It is a non-debt capital flow.
● FII (Foreign Institutional Investment).
● Foreign portfolio investment occurs, when foreign investment in the form
of shares, equities and bonds, is made by a foreign company.



● The three main institutions that handle FDI related issues in India are the
Foreign Investment Promotion Board (FIPB), the Foreign Investment
Implementation Authority (FIIA) and the Secretariat for Industrial Assistance
(S1A) activities/ sectors not opened to private sector, viz railways and atomic
energy.
●  Since. 20th May, 2011 FDI in Limited Liability Partnership (LLP) has
been allowed.

BANKING SYSTEM IN INDIA
●  Banking system occupies an important place in a nation’s economy. A
banking institution is indispensable in a modern society. It plays a pivotal
role in the economic development of a country and forms the core of the
money market in an advanced country.
SCHEDULED COMMERCIAL BANKS IN INDIA
The commercial banking structure in India consists of :
● Scheduled Commercial Banks in India
● Unscheduled Banks in India
● “Scheduled banks in India” bank being a bank included in the Second
Schedule to the Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 (2 of 1934), but does not
include a co-operative bank”.
● “Non-scheduled bank in India” those banks which are not included in
the second shedule of RBI, 1934 as Non-scheduled Bank.
● Local Area Bank : Announced in the Union Budget 1996-97 to ensure a
focussed saving and credit Mobilisation by defining the clear boundary of
operation, the Local Area Bank operates to a narrow geographical area of
three contiguous districts. The private sector is also allowed entry in the
segment.
Regional Rural Bank (RRB)
The RRBs were established since the 1975, with objectives of developing
rural economy by providing credit and deposits facilities for agriculture and
other productive activities of all kinds in rural areas. The equity of the RRBs
is contributed by the central Government, concerned state Government and
the sponsor bank in proportion of 50 : 15 : 35. There are 56 RRBs at present.
RESERVE BANK OF INDIA
● Reserve Bank of India (RBI) is the Central Bank of India. It was established
in April 1935 under Reserve Bank of India Act, 1934 with authorised capital
of 5 crore. It was nationalised on January 1, 1949. It is fully owned by the



government of India. It is governed by a central board (headed by Governor)
appointed by the Central Government.
●  The Management of RBI rests with the Central Board of Directors
consisting of 14 Directors besides the one Governer and Four Deputy
Governer.
● The office of Local boards — New Delhi, Kolkata, Mumbai, Chennai.
● These local boards function according to the Central Board.
Functions of RBI –
1. Issue of bank Notes. 2. Banker to the Government
3. Banker’s Bank 4. Controller of Credit
5. Custodian of foreign exchange reserves
6. Arrangement of agriculture credit.
7. Provide Facility of Clearing House to banks.
8. Supervisory functions.
9. Collecting and publishing economic data.
10. Selling of Government securities and trade bills.
Instruments of Credit Control Quantitative Measures
● Bank Rate  ● Cash Reserve Ratio  ● Repo Rate  ● Reverse Repo Rate  ● 
Statutory Liqudity Ratio  ● Open Market Operations
Qualitative Measures
● Rationing of credit
● Direct Action
(A) Quantitative Measures :
(1) Bank Rate : It is the rate at which the RBI is willing to lend to the
commercial banks. It is not the main tool to control money supply these days.
Bank rate Liquidity Demand Inflation control Bank rate Liquidity Demand
out from recession
(2) Cash Reserve Ratio (CRR) : Commercial banks are required to keep a
certain amount of cash reserves at the central bank (RBI). This percentage
amount is called cash reserve Ratio. CRR is maintained only in cash form
with RBI.
(3) Repo Rate : It is the rate at which banks borrow rupees from RBI. Repo
rate is an instrument under Liquidity Adjustment Facility (LAF) at which
RBI lends to commercial banks.
● Repo rate Liquidity Interest Demand Repo rate Liquidity Interest
(4) Reverse Repo Rate : It is a rate at which RBI borrows from commercial
banks.



● In case of inflationary tendencies, RBI can hike the reverse Repo Rate to
absorb the excess liquidity in the market.
Reverse Repo Rate Liquidity Price
(5) Statutory liquidity Ratio (SLR)
● Commercial banks are also required to keep a certain percentage of their
net demand and time liabilities (NDTL) as liquid assets in the shape of cash,
gold or approved securities.
(6) Liquid Adjustment facility (LAF) : It is a monetary police tool which
allows banks to borrow money through repurchase agreements.
● LAF consists of repo and reverse repo operations.
(7) Open Market operations : When RBI starts buying or selling
government securities to control money supply
(8) Marginal Standing Facility (MSF)
● MSF is the penal rate which banks can borrow money from the Central
Bank by pledging government securities within the limits of statutory
Liquidity Ratio (SLR).
(B) Qualitative Measures—
● Fixing minimum margins for lending against specific securities.
● Ceilings on amounts of credit for certain purpose.
● Discriminatory rates of interest for certain types of advances.
●  Cash Authorisation Scheme—Under this scheme, all commercial banks
have to obtain RBI’s authorisation before sanctioning any fresh credit above
certain limit.
●  Selective controls are focussed mainly on credit to traders for financing
inventories (for purposes of hoarding and speculation). In recent years,
selective controls have been used with great effect.
Printing Press
1. Indian Security Press — Nasik (Maharashtra)
2. Security Printing Press – Hyderabad (Telangana)
3. Currency Press Note – Nasik (Maharashtra)
● Printing of Rs. 10, 50, 100, 500 and 1000 notes and supply.

Capital Market
Capital markets are markets for buying and selling equity and debt
instruments.
Capital market channel savings and investment between suppliers of capital
such as retail investors and institutional investors, and users of capital like
business, government and individuals. Capital market are vital to the
functioning of an economy, since capital is a critical component for
generating economic output. Capital markets include primary markets, where
new stock and bond issues are sold to investors, and secondary markets,
where trading of existing securities.
Demat Trading
●  Trading in shares, bonds, debentures in electronic mode through the
depositors mechanism.
● There are two depositories : NSDL & CSDL NSDL (National Securities
Depositories Ltd.) 1996 CSDL (Central Depositories Services Ltd.) 1998
Mutual Funds —
● These funds are sizeable collections of savings by a no. of investors and
are managed by a form of specialists (who make up for individual investor’s
lack of knowledge of capital market).
They are marked by relative safety and optimum returns to investors.
● Set up by public sector banks, financial institutions and private corporate
sector, under the guidelines by SEBI.
Mutual Funds in India —
●  Mutual fund is an instrument of investing money in stock market. A
mutual fund is a group of investors operating through a fund manager to
purchase a diverse portfolio of stocks or bonds. Mutual funds are highly cost
efficient and very very to invest in.
Stock Exchange
● Stock exchanges in the country—
● Regional includes Bombay Stock Exchange (BSE)
●  National only three— (1) National Stock Exchange (NSE), Bombay (2)
Over the Counter Exchange of India Ltd. (OTCEI) (3) Inter Connected Stock
Exchange of India Ltd. (ISE)



● Phelwani Committee (1991) recommended for establishment of NSE in
1992, Government authorized IDBI for establishing NSE. Thus IDBI is the
main promoter of NSE.
●  Bull : A spectacular on the stock exchange or on a commodity market,
who buys shares in the anticipation of rising prices so that they can be later
resold at a profit.
● Bear : A spectacular who sells shares in anticipation of falling prices to
buy then back and thus make a profit.
● Footsie is ‘Financial Times Stock Exchange 100 share Index.’ Published
by Financial Times of London. MIBOR (Mumbai Inter-bank Offer Rate)–
Landing rate for loans MIBIR (Mumbai Inter-bank Bid Rate) – Landing rate
for recipients
Bombay Stock Exchange
●  Bombay Stock Exchange Limited is the oldest stock exchange in Asia.
Popularly known as BSE it was established as ‘The Native Share & Stock
Brokers Assiciation’ in 1875. It is the first stock exchange in India to obtain
permanent recognition in 1956 from the Government of India under the
securities Controls (Regulation) Act, 1956.
Securities and Exchange Board of India (SEBI)—
● Set up as non-statutory body in April, 1988
● Given statutory status in 1992
Functions :
● Oversee the working of stock exchanges
● Regulate merchant banks and mutual funds.
● Register and regulate intermediaries such as stock brokers.
● Curb fraudulent and unfair trade practices including insider tradings.
● Promote the development of a healthy capital market.
Money Market
● While the Government securities market generally caters to the investors
with a long-term investment horizon, the money market provides investment
avenues of short term tenor.
● Money market transactions are generally used for funding the transactions
in other markets including Government securities as market and meeting
short form liquidity mismatches.
● By definition, money market is for a maximum tenor of up to one year.
Within the one year, depending upon the tenors, money market is classified



into :
(1) Overnight market — the tenor of transactions is one working day.
(2) Notice money market—The tenor of transactions is from 2 days to 14
days.
(3) Term money market—The tenor of the transactions is from 15 days to
one year.
● NEFT : National Electronic Funds Transfer is a nation wide system that
facilitates individuals, firms and corporates to electronically transfer funds
from any bank branch to any individual, firm or corporate having an account
with any other bank branch in the country.
●  IFSC : Indian Financial System Code is an alpha-numeric code that
uniquely identifies a bank branch participating in the NEFT system. This is a
11 digit code with first 4 alpha characters representing the bank, and the last
6 numeric characters representing the branch. The 5th Character is zero.
INSURANCE SECTOR :
●  An Ordinance was issued on 19th January 1956 nationalising the Life
Insurance sector.
●  In 1972 with the passing of the General Insurance Business
(Nationalisation) Act, general insurance business was nationalized with
effect from 1st January 1973.
GIC holds four companies :
(i) National Insurance Company Ltd.
(ii) New India Assurance Company Ltd.
(iii) Oriental Fire and Insurance Company Ltd.
(iv) United India Insurance Company Ltd.
● The IROA opened up the market in August 2000 with the invitation for
application for registrations. he Authority has the power to frame regulations
under section 114-A of the Insurance Act 1938 and has framed various
regulations ranging from registration of companies to protection of
policyholders interests.
Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority (IRDA) :
● IRDA was set up in 2000 through Insurance Regulatory and Development 
Act, 1999.  Authority is responsible for the regulation, development and
supervision of the Indian insurance Industry.
 



GENERAL SCIENCE
###PHYSICS###

Physics is the branch of science that deals with the study of matter and
energy and their inter–relationship.
MOTION
● Motion is change in position of a body with time and a reference point.
● Types of Motion are, Linear Motion, Curvilinear and Rotatory Motion.
●  Scalar quantities : Those physical quantities which are completely
described by their magnitude (size) alone, are known as scalar quantities, e.g.
length or distance, mass, time, area, volume, work, power, energy,
temperature, speed, pressure, charge and potential.
●  Vector quantities : Those physical quantities which are completely
described only if their magnitudes as well as directions are known are called
vector quantities, e.g. displacement, velocity, acceleration, force, weight,
momentum and electric field.
DISTANCE
● The distance traveled by a body is the actual length of the path covered by
a moving body irrespective of the direction in which the body travels. It is a
scalar quantity having magnitude only.
DISPLACEMENT
● When a body moves from one position to another, the shortest distance
between the initial position and final position of the body along with
direction is known as its displacement. It is vector quantity having magnitude
as well as direction.
SPEED
● Speed is the scalar form of velocity. It is defined as the distance traveled in
one second.
● If the body covers a distance d in time t, then speed V = d/t.
AVERAGE SPEED
●  If a body has travelled ‘S1’ and ‘S2’ distance with ‘V1’ and ‘V2’
velocities, then, Average speed = Total distance/Total time



VELOCITY
● The rate of change of displacement is called velocity.
● V = s/t It is a vector quantity.
● Velocity may be positive and negative or zero depending on displacement.
UNIFORM VELOCITY
●  When a body covers equal displacement in equal interval of time, the
velocity is said to be uniform.
● Acceleration is absent if the body moves with uniform velocity as initial
velocity equals final velocity.
VARIABLE VELOCITY
● When a body covers unequal displacement in equal intervals of time then it
is said to have variable velocity.
● Acceleration is necessarily present in this motion.
● In this case either direction of velocity or magnitude or both change with
respect to time e.g. speed is constant in uniform circular motion but velocity
is variable with change in direction.
AVERAGE VELOCITY
1. Average velocity is zero if the body returns to starting point in the given
time interval.
2. If the body covers first half distance with velocity v1 and next half with
velocity v2 then the average velocity
● A body whose velocity reduces with the passage of time, it is said to be
undergoing deceleration or retardation.
● The acceleration of a moving body is the rate of change of its velocity, i.e.,
; where ‘a’ is the acceleration, ‘u’ is the initial velocity which becomes ‘v’ in
‘t’ second.
ACCELERATION
●  Time rate of change in velocity is known as acceleration. It is a vector
quantity.
● Negative acceleration is known as retardation. It indicates that the velocity
of the object is decreasing with respect to time as on application of brakes of
a body in motion.
VARIABLE ACCELERATION



● If the velocity of the body changes in different amounts during the same
time intervals, then the acceleration of the body is known as variable
acceleration.
● Acceleration is variable if either its direction or magnitude or both changes
with respect to time, e.g. a body in uniform circular motion is accelerated
motion.
UNIFORM ACCELERATION
● If the velocity of the body changes by same amounts in same intervals of
time, then the acceleration of the body is known as uniform acceleration, e.g.
acceleration due to gravity otherwise not such as a motor cyclist riding on a
busy city road.
UNIFORM CIRCULAR MOTION
● When a particle moves in a circle at a constant speed then motion is said to
be uniform circular motion.
●  In circular motion as position of particle changes so also its velocity.
Hence, the circular motion, is considered as accelerated motion.
● In circular motion acceleration which is always directed towards the centre
of circle is called centripetal acceleration.
EQUATIONS OF MOTION
(i) v = u + at, (ii) (iii) v2 = u2 + 2as; Where initial velocity = u Final velocity
= v Elapsed time = t Acceleration = a Distance/displacement covered = s
●  The acceleration caused by the earth’s attraction is known as the
acceleration due to gravity and it is identified by the symbol ‘g’. Mean value

of ‘g’ is 9.8 m/s2.
● If the body travels downwards, then ‘g’ is positive. However, if the body is
projected vertically upwards, then ‘g’ is negative.
LAWS OF MOTION
● Newton’s First Law of Motion :
Every body continues to be in a state of rest or of uniform motion, unless
compelled by an external force to change that state. This first law is also
called law of Inertia.
● Newton’s Second Law of Motion : The rate of change of momentum is
directly proportional to the applied force and takes place in the direction in



which the force acts.
● The second law can also be termed as the law of acceleration.
● Newton’s Third Law of Motion :
For every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction.
● The force acts on two different bodies in opposing directions.
FORCE AND INERTIA
● A force is that pull or push which changes or tends to change the state of
motion of a body or its shape.
● The tendency of a body to remain in the same state it is, either stationary or
moving, is called its inertia.
● The more the mass of the objects, the greater is their inertia.
● The force (F) which causes an acceleration (a) in a moving object of mass
(m) is given by F = ma

● The SI unit of force is Newton (kg.ms–2).
BANKING OF TRACKS
● For a vehicle to make a safe and easier turn roads on large highways are
generally banked i.e. road bend at the curved path is raised a little on the
sideways away from centre. With this banking of road, a component of
normal force points towards the centre of curvature of road. It supplies the
necessary centripetal force required for circular motion. The vertical
component is balanced by the weight of vehicle.
MOMENTUM
● The force which a body possesses, due to the combined effect of its mass
and velocity is called momentum (p = mv).
● The greater the mass and velocity of an object, the larger is its momentum.
● For example, cars are heavy and fast. Thus, they have enough momentum
to crumple steel and break down brick walls when they crash to a halt.
● According to the law of conservation of momentum, in a colliding system,
the total momentum remains conserved.
m1u1 + m2u2 = m1v1 + m2v2
● When a force of very large magnitude is applied on a body for a very short
interval of time, then collective effect of force and time is called impulse.
● It’s impulse that allows some people break bricks on a single hit.



GRAVITY
● Gravity is the force which pulls all bodies towards the centre of the earth,
keeps the planet revolving round the sun in definite paths and is responsible
for the weight of all objects.
● A falling body accelerates under the influence of gravity.
● Different masses, fall to the ground together, because acceleration due to
gravity is same for all objects.
●  Greater the masses and smaller the separation, greater is the force of
attraction. It is called Gravitational Force.
● The value of acceleration due to gravity, i.e., ‘g’ decreases both with height
and depth.
● The radius of the earth is slightly more at the equator than at the poles. So,
‘g’ is less at the equator than at the poles.
● Therefore, artificial satellites are launched from the places near or on the
equator.

ESCAPE VELOCITY It is the minimum velocity with which a body must
escape or projected away from earth surface such that it never return.
For earth it value is 11.2 km/sec and for moon = 2.4 km/sec.
MASS AND WEIGHT
● Mass is measure of the atoms contained in an object, and the number of
atoms in the object remains constant.
● So, mass is an intrinsic, unchanging property of matter.
● The force with which a body is attracted towards earth is called weight. W
= mg; where ‘W’ is the weight and ‘m’ is the mass.
● Mass is measured with the help of physical (beam) balance, while a spring
balance also called Newtonmeter measures the weight of an object. The unit
of mass is kg but that for weight is Newton (N).
CHANGES IN APPARENT WEIGHT IN ELEVATOR
●  Based on Newton’s Second law, when accelerating upward one feels
heavier and accelerating downward, one feels lighter. If the elevator’s cable
breaks, one would feel weightlessness because both elevator and the person
would accelerate downwards at the same rate.
FRICTION



●  Friction is a kind of force that opposes relative motion. It comes into
existence at the common boundary of two bodies in contact with one another,
when one of them either moves or tends to move relative to the other.
● Force of friction does not depend on the area of contact.
● Frictional force increases with weight.
● On rough surfaces, frictional force is more than that on the smooth surfaces
because of more interlocking.
●  Less force is needed to maintain motion than to initiate it i.e., Kinetic
friction < Static friction.
● The vehicles are provided with wheels and their axles are supported with
ball bearings, because the rolling friction is much less than the sliding
friction.
●  Friction is necessary for the operation of belts, pulleys, clutches and
brakes. Nails and screws remain firmly fixed in which they are driven
because of friction.
● Therefore, friction is considered as necessary evil.
WORK, POWER AND ENERGY
●  Work is said to be done only, when a force or its component causes
displacement in its own direction or stops a body in motion or changes the
body’s shape.

● In SI system, unit of work is called Joule. (kg m25–2)
● W = F × d; where ‘W’ is the work done, ‘F’ is the force and ‘d’ is the
displacement.
●  Rate of doing work is called power. Power (P) can be defined as the
product of force (f) and velocity (v). P = Fv
●  In SI system, absolute unit of power, work done/time is called

Joules/second (JS–1) or Watt or 1 kw = 1000 w.
● The capacity of a body to do work is called energy.
● Energy is the total amount of work done by a body, therefore, its unit is the
same as that of work (Joule).
●  Conveniently, energy is divided into (i) potential energy and (ii) kinetic
energy.
● When a body does work by virtue of its motion, it is said to have kinetic
energy. If ‘m’ is the mass of the body, moving with an initial velocity v, then



its Kinetic Energy (K.E.) is given by K.E. = 1/2 mv2
● If a body of mass ‘m’ be raised vertically through a height ‘h’ against the
acceleration due to gravity ‘g’, then its Potential Energy (P.E.) is P.E. = mgh.
● When a body is on the surface of earth, then the potential energy between
the system of body, and the earth is zero.
● When the body moves vertically upward, i.e., the height of the body from
ground increases, the potential energy between the system of the earth and
the body also increases.
●  Satellites : There are two type of satellites remote sensing, weather
forecasting satellites etc.
mostly placed close to earth at height of 800 km or above.
●  Telecommunication satellites also called geostationary satellite which
appear stationary are placed at height of 36,000 km from earth surface.
Kepler’s Laws of planetary motion :
(i) All planets are revolving elliptical orbit.
(ii) All planets sweeps equal area is equal interval of time when revolving
around the sun.
(iii) The square of time period of a planet around the sun is proportional to
the cube of the semi major axis of the ellipse.
INTERCONVERSION OF ENERGY
● When hands are rubbed, the mechanical energy due to friction changes into
heat energy.
●  When two stones are struck, the mechanical energy changes to heat and
light energy.
● When brakes are applied, the mechanical energy changes to heat energy at
the point where brakes rub against moving wheel.
●  When an arrow is stretched in a bow, the mechanical energy changes to
potential energy.
●  On releasing string, the potential energy changes to kinetic energy of
arrow.
●  The water stored in dams has gravitational potential energy. When this
water is released, it changes to kinetic energy of flowing water.
● The kinetic energy of flowing water turns blades of turbine and changes to
mechanical energy.
●  This mechanical energy of turbine drives dynamo to generate electricity
i.e., changes to electric energy.



●  When a torch is switched on, the chemical energy changes to electric
energy. The electric energy on flowing through filament of bulb changes to
heat and light energy.
FLUID PRESSURE
●  Pressure is defined as the thrust acting per unit area of the surface of
contact i.e., P = F/A
● Where ‘P’ is the pressure, ‘F’ is the thrust and ‘A’ is the area over which
the thrust is acting. Thrust is force that acts normal to surface. The SI unit of

pressure is known as pascal (Nm–2).
● The smaller the area on which force acts, greater is its impact e.g., if an
elephant steps on a persons chest, ribs would crack but in circus a person
shows the same with a light but strong wooden blank placed on chest
because of large area.
● Pascal’s law states that a change in pressure is applied to an enclosed fluid
is transmitted undiminished to every point of the fluid and walls of
containing vessel. A fluid means a liquid or gas.
● Hence, the law states that a liquid exerts pressure equally in all directions.
●  Hydraulic breaks, flying of an airplane are based on the pressure and
Bernaullies principle.
●  Archimedes’ Principle : When a body is immersed in a liquid fully or
partially, it suffers an upthrust which is equal to the weight of liquid
displaced by the immersed part of it.
● The result of the upthrust is an apparent loss of weight of the body when
immersed in the liquid.
●  Therefore a body weighs more in air then when partally or wholly
immersed in water.
● Surface Tension : It is a scalar physical quantity being unidirectional is
defined as the property of surface of a liquid by which it tends to contract
and occupy the minimum possible surface area i.e. the upper surface acts as a
stretched membrane.
● With rise in temperature it decreases except in case of molten copper and
cadmium.
Tsolid > Tliquid > Tgas with tags = 0
● With addition of any impurity surface tension decreases if the impurity is
sparing in soluble in water e.g soap added to water.



● But if increases if impurity is soluble like common salt or sugar added to
water.
● When a wire bent in form of a ring is clopped into soap solution, and then
taken out, a film is formed due to surface tensions.
● Surface tension of liquid metals like that of mercury is very high.
● An egg floats in water because of surface tension. It acts as if placed on a
stretched membrance.
● Capillarity : It refers to rise or depression of a liquid in a capillary tube
● It’s application involves
(1) Rising of oil/molten wax through wick in a candle.
(2) Action of towel in soaking up moisture from body.
(3) Absorption of water by root hair in soil.
● Hydro dynamics : It is the science of fluids in motion.
● Principle of continuity :
According to principle, in case of steady flow of incompressible and non-
viscous fluid through a tube of non-uniform cross section, the product of area
of cross section and velocity of flow remains same at every point in tube.
AV = constant The velocity of flow increases with decrease in area of cross
section. Therefore, deep water appear to be still and falling stream of water
becomes same.
● Viscosity : It is the property of a fluid because of which it opposes the
relative motion between different layers and the acting force is called viscous
force. It depends on nature of fluid and is independent of area. viscosity of
liquids > viscosity of gases, glass being the most viscous.
viscosity increases with density for liquids, but with rise in temperature it
decreases for liquids, with increasing pressure of liquid, it increases but
decreases in case of gases.
HEAT AND THERMODYNAMICS
●  Heat is a form of energy that flows from one body to another due to
temperature difference between them. It is the combined energy of all
molecules moving inside a body.
● Temperature is only a measure of how fast these molecules are moving.
The faster the molecules in a body vibrate, the hotter it is.
● The flow of heat from one body to another continues until both the sides
are at the same temperature.
● The two bodies are then said to be in ‘Thermal Equilibrium’.



● In general, matter (whether solid, liquid or gas) expands when heated and
contracts when cooled except water which shows anomalous behaviour. It
expands on cooking with reducing density. It is this property of water that
allows aquatic animals to survive on freezing of water in lakes or rivers.
●  Black or dark bodies absorb heat while white or bright, shiny surfaces
reflect away the heat.
● Black colours are good absorber and emitter of heat.
● Kelvin is the SI unit of temperature. The temperature is expressed in kelvin
and not in degree kelvin.
TRANSMISSION OF HEAT
● The process of net energy transfer through a substance without movement
of the substance itself is called conduction.
● Specific heat : The amount of heat required to change the temperature of a
body.
● Thermal Capacity : It is the quantity of heat energy required to raise the
temperature of body by unit.
Thermal capacity = mass of body × specific heat
● Water equivalent : It refers to amount of water that absorbs or gives out
the same amount of heat as is done by body when heated by 1°C.
●  Latent heat : The amount of heat energy gained or lost when a body
undergoes a change in its phase from solid to liquid to gas or viceversa as
latest heat of fusion or latest heat of vapourization.
●  Transmission of heat : It occure through conduction, convection or
radiation.
● The process of transfer of heat energy within the heated medium by the
movement of the substance within itself is known as convection.
● The process of transfer of heat from one body to another without requiring
any medium is called radiation.
● Very poor conductors, such as glass and plastics, are called insulators.
●  All substances expand on heating exerting more pressure on side walls
hence expand.
Newton Law of cooling : rate of cooling of a hot body is directly
proportional to temperature difference between the body and its surrounding
i.e. amount of heat lost = amount of gained by other body.
● Black body : A body that absorbs all the radiations incident on its surface
is called a perfect black body should neither reflect nor turns unit any



radiation which is not 100% possible. It always appear black despite of the
colour of radiation incident its surface.
MAGNETISM
● Magnetism is the branch of physics concerned with magnetic phenomenon.
● Materials can be divided broadly into three categories.
(i) Diamagnetic Substances
(ii) Paramagnetic Substances
(iii) Ferromagnetic Substances
●  Diamagnetic substances are those substances which are repelled by a
magnet. e.g. copper, lead, gold, zinc, tin, water, air, antimony.
●  Paramagnetic substances are attracted by a magnet very feebly, e.g.,
Aluminium, Chromium, Oxygen, Platinum.
●  Ferromagnetic substances are attracted by magnets and can also be
magnetised. e.g. Iron, Cobalt, Nickel.
● Ferromagnetic substances behave just like paramagnetic materials but the
effect is much more intense.
Paramagnetic Diamagnetic Ferromagnetic
● The area around a magnet in which one magnetic strength of a magnet is
experienced, is called magnetic field.
● In a bar magnet, the magnetic strength is maximum at the corner rejoins
called poles than in the middle of magnet.
SOME IMPORTANT FACTS ABOUT MAGNETISM
● The regions of strongest magnetisation near the ends of a magnet are called
poles of the magnet.
● The distance between the two poles of a magnet is known as the effective
length of the magnet.
●  An imaginary line bisecting the effective length of the magnet, at right
angles to it, is called the magnetic equator of the magnet.
●  The induced magnetism acquired by soft iron is temporary, whereas the
induced magnetism acquired by steel is more or less permanent i.e., steel has
greater retentivity and it has higher coercivity i.e., resistance to lose
magnetism than iron.
●  Magnetic materials which retain magnetism for considerable time are
known as hard materials and those which lose magnetism easily are called
soft materials.
● Iron is an example of a soft material while steel is a hard material.



● The rapid spinning of the Earth creates electric currents in the molten core,
which are responsible for the magnetic field around the earth.
● Repulsion, and not attraction, is the surest test for polarity of a magnet.
● Certain types of iron, which once magnetised retain their magnetism, are
called permanent magnets.
●  An insulated copper wire wounded on some cylindrical card board or
plastic tube, such that its length is greater than its diameter and it behaves
like a magnet when current is made to flow through is a solenoid.
●  Curie temperature : It refers to the temperature at which a given
substance changes its magnetic property for example form ferromagnetic to
paramagnetic etc.
● Galvanometer is an instrument used for :
(i) detecting the flow of current in an electric circuit, (ii) finding the direction
of flow of current, (iii) measuring the strength of current, (iv) making
measuring devices, such as voltmeter and ammeter.
ELECTRICITY
●  Electricity is the phenomenon associated with stationary or moving
electrons, ions or other charged particles.
● The electricity produced by friction is called frictional electricity.
●  If the charges in a body do not move, then the frictional electricity is
known as static electricity i.e., study of charges at rest.
●  The electricity i.e., charges that travels along wires is called current
electricity.
● An electric current is defined as an electric charge in motion. The unit of
current is ampere and it is measured with the help of an instrument called
ammeter.
●  The opposition or obstruction offered by a conductor to the passage of
drifting electrons is called electric resistance. It’s unit is ohm.
●  Resistance of conductor is directly proportional to its length, inversely
proportional to its area of cross-section and upon the nature of conductor.
● Resistance of pure metals increases with the rise in temperature.
FACTS TO REMEMBER
● Fuse is a safety device in an electrical circuit. It is the weakest point in an
electrical circuit,which melts and breaks the electric circuit, when the circuit
gets :



(i) overloaded due to large withdrawal of current, (ii) due to short-circuiting
in the electric circuit, (iii) due to fluctuations of current in power supply
system.
● Fuse wire has a low melting point, and high resistance.
● Earthing protects the user and the appliance from electric shock and burn
out respectively.
● Household distribution of electricity is done in parallel, so that :
(i) if there is short-circuit or overloading in one particular circuit, then only
the fuse of that circuit will melt, but the power supply to other circuits
remains unaffected.
● Switches are always placed in live wire, so that an appliance is completely
cut off from live wire in off position that carry charges (electrons) with
energy.
● Thus, a user will not receive any accidental shock.
●  According to new international convention for colour coding of electric
wires, Red or brown is for live wire, light blue or black for neutral wire
and green or yellow for earth wire.
● Nichrome and Manganin are the common alloys used for making heating
elements of electric appliances, because they have good resistivity, high
melting point, low thermal expansion and do not get oxidised till 1000°C.
● In most of the cases, nichrome is used as heating element, e.g., in electric
iron, electric heater, geyser etc.
● The filament of electric bulb is made up of tungsten.
● The bulb is filled with a mixture of nitrogen and argon gases at very low
pressure. They being chemically inert.
DIRECT CURRENT AND ALTERNATIVE CURRENT
●  Direct Current or DC is that whose magnitude remains constant as a
function of time.
●  An Alternating Current or AC is that which varies in magnitude
continuously and whose direction varies periodically.
● Dry cells or batteries are source of d.c. but generator or power station is
that of a.c.
ELECTRIC MOTOR
●  When a current carrying rectangular conductor is placed in a magnetic
field at right angles to it, the forces acting on the two parallel lengths will be
equal and opposite thus constituting a couple.



● The conductor therefore rotate as per Flemings left hand rule. This is the
principle of electric motor.
DYNAMO It works on the reverse principle of the motor, i.e. Electro-
magnetic induction i.e., when a coil wrapped with wire is rotated between
opposite poles of two strong magnets, an e.m.f. is induced in the coil based
on Fleming's Right Hand rule.
TRANSFORMER
●  Transformer is an electric device, by which the e.m.f. of an alternating
current can be increased or decreased as per need of a particular situation.
COULOMB’S LAW
●  Force of attraction or repulsion between two point charges is directly
proportional to the product of the magnitude of charges and inversely
proportional to the square of the distance between them, acting in the
direction of the line joining the points. In SI system N
INTENSITY OF ELECTRIC FIELD
● The force experienced by a point charge of 1 coulomb in any electric field
is called the intensity of the–electric field at that point.

It is a vector quantity and its unit is Unit – N/C (NC–1)
ELECTRIC POTENTIAL (V) It is defined as the amount of work done
(W) in bringing a unit positive charge (q) from infinity to any electric field.
POTENTIAL DIFFERENCE
● The amount of work required in moving a charge of 1 Coulomb from a
point to the another is called the potential difference between those points.
OHM’S LAW
● The amount of electric current passing through any conductor is directly
proportional to the potential difference between its both ends i.e. V ∝ I or V
= IR (R is constant of proportionality) at any given temperature.
ATOMIC AND NUCLEAR PHYSICS
● Rutherford first invented the nucleus.
● X rays are produced when high speed electrons fall on tungsten.
● Radioactivity was discovered by ‘Henry Becquerel.’
