Ionising power
● Photo electric effect is the phenomenon of emission of electrons from a
metal surface when light of suitable frequency becomes incident on it.
●  The process by which a heavy radioactive nuclei is broken into lighter
nuclei by the bombardment of slow moving neutron, so as to liberate energy
and more neutrons than used for bombardment is called nuclear fission.
●  Chain reaction is the phenomenon in which neutrons liberated during
fission process lead to further fission of atoms, releasing a large amount of
energy.
●  If the fission process is carried out under controlled conditions, i.e., the
neutron formed during fission are absorbed by the control rods made up of
graphite and boron, then the energy so released can be utilised in producing
steam which can be further used in running turbines to generate electricity
such as in nuclear power stations.
● The process of combining lighter nuclei (atomic weight less than 20) into
heavier nuclei is called nuclear fusion. It is for this particular high
temperature, the fusion reaction are sometimes called thermonuclear
reaction.



●  The principle of nuclear fusion is used in Hydrogen bomb and energy
irradiated by sun and stars.
● Geiger counter is used for measuring radioactivity.
●  The radiation which is omnipresent on the earth because of radioactive
substances and cosmic ray particles are called background radiation.
● Radioactive property of carbon is used in carbon-14 dating to determine
the age of fossils.
Uses of radio isotopes :
P-32 : Treatment of skin diseases, lukemia, blood cancer.
J-134 : Treatment of thyriod gland.
Na-24 : Treatment of defects in blood circulation.
Co-60 : Treatment of Cancer C-14 : Estimating age of fossils.
● Half life : It is the time after which the amount of radioactive element
becomes half of the initial mass after releasing radiations. It is based on

principle E = mc2 in which mass is converted/lost to produce energy. ‘C’ is
the speed of light.
● Uranium obtained from earth crust can not directly be used as fuel. It needs

to be enriched i.e., U238
● In nuclear reactor instead of uranium, thorium or polonium can also be
used as nuclear fuels.
● Nuclear reactor : A nuclear reactor consists of five main components:

● Fuel : Some common fuels are uranium isotopes U233, U235, thorium

isotopes Th232 and plutonium isotop Pu 239.
● Moderator : Used to slow down the emitted neutrons. Commonly used
moderators are graphite, heavy water, beryllium, beryllium oxide and some
organic liquids.
● Coolant : Used to remove excessive heat developed inside the reactor core
some commonly used coolants are water, steam, helium, CO2, air, molten
metals etc.
● Control rods : Used to initiate or stop the nuclear fission reactions of the
nuclear reactor.
Cadmium and boron rods are used as control rods.
● In nuclear fission, a bigger nuclei is broken into smaller nuclei and there is
a huge amount of energy released.



● Atom Bomb and nuclear bomb work on nuclear fission.
●  When two or more smaller nuclei are jointed together to form a large
nuclei, the process is called nuclear fusion.
● A great amount of energy is released. Hydrogen bomb works on principle
of nuclear fusion.
● Nuclear fusion is the source of the Solar energy.
●  Cadmium is a good absorber of neutrons, used as controller in atomic
reaction.
● Nuclear reactor is based on controlled chain reaction.
SPECIFIC HEAT OF WATER
● Ice has the highest specific latent heat. So, compared to other matter, it
requires higher amount of energy for melting. That’s why;
(i) Water bodies in cold countries do not freeze suddenly, (ii) Snow melts
slowly on the mountains in the summers, due to which, there are no flash
floods, and the rivers contain water for whole year, (iii) Soft drinks are
cooled by ice rather than iced cold water.
● Boiling point of a liquid rises with presence of dissolved impurities.
● Therefore sometimes salt is added to slow down the melting of ice.
● The phenomenon due to which, a liquid changes into its vapour state at any
temperature, without the aid of any external source of heat is called
evaporation. Evaporation is basically the internal property of a liquid and it
produces cooking effect.
OPTICS
● Light is the physical cause which produces sensation of sight and makes
the surrounding objects visible to us.
● Bodies which themselves emit light are called luminous objects.
● The luminous objects are usually hot. However, cold luminous objects also
exist. The sun and the stars are examples of hot luminous objects, whereas,
the light emitting diodes used in digital instruments, fire fly and electric ray
fish are examples of cold luminous objects.
● Reflection is the phenomenon whereby light, on striking an opaque surface
is thrown back into the same medium provided that angle of incidence is
equal to angle of reflection with respective at normal down at point of
incidence.
● An opaque body absorbs and transmit some of the light that falls on it but
majorly reflects the remaining light.



● A transparent body like glass or water transmits almost all the light that
passes through it.
● A smooth shining surface like a mirror reflects away almost all the light
incident on it.
MIRRORS
● The image formed by a plane mirror is erect, laterally inverted and virtual.
Also, it has the same size as the object and is as far behind the mirror as the
object is in front of it.
● If a person approaches a plane mirror at a distance with speed of 1m/s then
image would approach the person at speed of 2m/s.
● A polished surface is visible only from that direction in which it reflects
the light.
●  In reflection from irregular surfaces, the object can be seen from all
directions.
●  A man can see the whole of his body in a mirror provided the size of
mirror is minimum half of his own height.
● A set of two parallel mirrors produces theoretically an infinite number of
images. However, in practice only a limited number of images are observed,
since each successive image is fainter than the preceeding one.
●  The portion of the mirror from where reflection actually takes place is
known as aperture.
●  In a concave mirror, the image is always real except when the object is
within the focal length.
● In the latter case, the image is virtual, erect and magnified.
● In a convex mirror, irrespective of the position of the object, the image is
always behind the mirror. It is always virtual, small size and the image
distance from the mirror is always less than the object’s distance.
● The image formed by a concave mirror might be an enlarged or diminished
one depending upon the position of the object.
● But in a convex mirror, the image is always a diminished image.
● Image formation by concave mirror

● A concave mirror is used as shaving mirror, Doctor’s reflector, reflectors of
search light, in terrestrial telescope etc.
● A convex mirror or a diverging mirror is used as driving mirror, anti
● A convex mirror or a diverging mirror is used as driving mirror, anti theft
mirror in malls and street lamp reflector.
FACTS TO REMEMBER
● The phenomenon, due to which a ray of light deviates from its original
path, while traveling from one optical medium to another optical medium of
different density is called refraction.
● When a ray of light travels from optically rarer medium to optically denser
medium, it always bends towards normal, drawn at the point of incidence.
● Normal is the perpendicular drawn at the point of incidence on interface.
● When a ray of light travels from optically denser medium (glass or water)
to optically rarer medium (air or vaccum), it always bends away from the
normal, drawn at the point of incidence.
● When an incident ray strikes at right angles at the point of incidence, it
suffers no refraction.
● The perpendicular shift in the path of incident ray,while emerging out from
an optical slab is called lateral displacement.
● Because of refraction :
(i) A stick appears bent and short when immersed obliquely in water. It also
appears magnified because image is formed close to the eye.



(ii) The bottom of a beaker filled with water appears raised.
Swimming pool appears shallow when filled with water.
(iii) Stars twinkle and appear higher in horizon than their actual positon.
(iv) The Sun appears bigger during sunset or sunrise. Atmospheric refraction
is responsible for twilight and helps in increasing the length of the day by
four minutes, difference between real and apparent day length.
●  The phenomenon due to which a ray of light, while traveling from
optically denser medium to optically rarer medium, gets reflected into the
same medium at the surface of separation, is called total internal reflection
(TIR).
● For total internal reflection to take place :
(i) rays of light must travel form optically denser medium to optically rarer
medium, (ii) the angle of incidence in optically denser medium, must be
greater than critical angle.
● Angle of incidence in a denser medium for which angle of refraction in
rarer medium is 90° is called Critical Angle.
●  Sparkling of diamonds and formation of mirage take place due to total
internal reflection. It is also used in optical fibres for endoscopy or in
communication.
LENSES
● A concave lens can be used as :
(i) eye lens for Gallelio telescope, and
(ii) correcting short sightedness.
● A convex lens always forms real and inverted images, except it forms a
virtual magnified and erect image, when object is between optical centre and
principal focus. It's when, it is used as magnifying lens.

● A concave lens always forms virtual, erect and diminished image.
● The power of lens is the measure of convergence or divergence produced
by it and is measured in dioptres (D).

HUMAN EYE
● In human eye, cornea, acts as window to the world, i.e., allows the light to
enter the eye. It causes maximum refraction of light entering into an eye.
● In human eye, choroid darkens the eye from inside and hence prevents any
internal reflection.
●  The function of retina consisting of rod and cone cells is to receive the
optical image of an object and then convert it into optical pulses which are
finally sent to brain through optic nerve.
● Ciliary muscles alter the focal length of crystalline lens, so that images of
objects at various distances are clearly focused on retina. The property of eye
lens to focus image on retina is called power of accomodation.
● Iris of the eye controls the amount of light entering into the eye. It acts like
the adjustable diaphragm of the camera. It imparts colour to eye because of
pigment present in it.



● Blind spot is the area on retina from where optic nerve originates and leave
the eye. No image is formed if focused on it and the person behave as blind
person.
● Eye-lids of human eye act as the shutter in front of camera to restrict light.
EYE DEFECTS
●  The common principal causes of eye defects are myopia and
hypermetropia.

● The other eye defects include old age hypermetropia also called presbyopia
and astigmatism caused by roughening of cornea hence, corrected by
cylindrical lenses.
● A convex lens of short focal length is used as magnifying glass or simple
microscope.
● A compound microscope uses lens of short aperture and small focal length
as objective lens and other lens of short focal length but large aperture as eye
piece lens.

Ray diagram for the formation of image by a compound microscope
● An astronomical telescope uses objective lens of large aperture and large
focal length and eye-piece of small aperture and small focal length.



Astronomial telescope
● A binocular consists of a pair of astronomical telescope whose tube length
is reduced using prism.
●  Interference of light is the phenomenon of non-uniform distribution of
energy in a medium because of superposition of two light waves from a
coherent source placed closed to each other. It is explained by Young's
Double Slit experiment.
● Diffraction is the bending of light round the sharp corners before spreading
into the region provided that the size of hole is comparable to wave length of
light. The observation of a coloured spectrum of light when watching across
a muslin cloth is because of diffraction.
● Polarisation of light refers to the restriction of the vibrations of light in one
plane. It is obtained by passing light through Nicol’s prism or tourmaline
crystal.
Polaroids are used in making good sun glasses than coloured glasses, shields
of automobiles, and window panes of a train or airplane.
●  The ordinary light from a source is transverse unpolarised light that is
polarised by polarizer.
SOUND AND WAVE MOTION
●  Sound is longitudinal wave and requires a material medium for
transmission and propagation.
● A transverse wave is one in which the motion of the individual particles of
the medium takes place at right angles to the direction of propagation of the
wave.
● Transverse waves can be generated in solids and liquids but not in gases.
● A longitudinal wave is the one in which the motion of individual particles
of the medium takes place in the direction of propagation of the wave,
resulting in the formation of alternate compressions and rarefactions.
● Longitudinal waves can be produced in solids, liquids and gases.
● Light waves are transverse in nature, while sound waves are longitudinal
waves.



● The speed of sound is independent of loudness, provided the amplitude is
not very large.
● The velocity of sound is independent of pressure.
● Sound travels faster in warm air.
● The velocity of sound increases if the wind is blowing in the direction of
sound wave propagation and vice-versa.
● Sounds of all frequencies travel with the same speed.
● Speed of sound is more in water than in air and still more in solids like
steel.
● The phenomenon due to which repetition of sound is heard, after reflection
from distant object (such as high building or hillock), after the original sound
from a given source dies off is called ‘Echo’.
● SONAR, sound navigation and ranging is based on reflection of sound that
causes echo.
●  These days reflection of sound (ultrasound) technique is widely used in
medical sciences to diagnose the presence of tumour or health of foetus in a
mother or presence of a stone in a body organ.
●  Resonance is defined as a phenomenon when the frequency of applied
external force is equal to the natural frequency of the body on which force is
applied, such that it readily takes up the vibrations and begins to vibrate with
an increased amplitude.
●  Pitch is the characteristic of musical sound, which enables us to
differentiate between two sounds of equal loudness, coming with different
frequencies from two sources.
● The higher the freqeuncy of a note, the higher is its pitch or the shriller is
its note. Among humans, pitch of a female's sound is higher than that of a
male member.
● Waves and frequencies : Audible range 20Hz – 20,000 Hz, generated by
vibrating bodies such as vocal cords. Audiblic to human. At NTP,
wavelength range-16.5 m to 1.65 cm.
●  Infrasonic range : Frequency below 20Hz, produced by earth quakes.
volcanic eruptions or ocean waves. Mostly longtitudinal mechanical waves.
●  Ultrasonic range : Above 20,000 KHz, longituduinal mechanical waves,
produced by high frequency quartz crystals, fishes, mosquitos bats. Used for
navigation (SONAR) or bloodless surgery and wave theraphy
● Velocity of sound : Vsteel > Vneter > Vair.
with increase in temperature of air, velocity of sound also increases.



● RADAR : System of locating distant objects by means of the reflected
radio waves usually of micro wave frequency.
● The rate of flow of sound energy per unit is called intensity of sound. The
rate of flow of sound energy per unit area and its effect on the ear
collectively is called loudness of sound.
● Light waves are transverse electromagnetic waves.
● Radio waves, television waves, microwaves, infra–red rays, visible light,
ultra–violet rays, x-rays and gamma rays are major parts of electromagnetic
spectrum in the order of decreasing wavelength or increasing frequency.
●  Radio waves, TV-waves and micro-waves are produced by oscillating
circuits in special vacuum tubes.
● Infra-red rays, visible radiation and ultra-violet rays are produced by the
excitation of outer electronic shell in increasing order.
● X-rays are produced by the excitation of inner electrons of an atom and
sudden destruction of high energy free electrons.
● Gamma radiation are produced by the nucleus of an atom, by the sudden
destructions of high energy particles. They find use in the cure of cancer like
the one released by Co-60.


###CHEMISTRY###

Chemistry is the branch of science concerned with the composition,
properties, characteristics and reactions of substances along with energy
changes. Inorganic Chemistry is associated with the properties of
compounds of the chemical elements contained in minerals.
Organic Chemistry is concerned with the compounds of carbon and
hydrogen or their derivatives.
STATES OF MATTER
● Anything that occupies space and has mass or weight is called Matter.
●  Matter undergoes changes which may be either chemical changes or
physical changes.
● All matters exist in one of the three physical forms or states: solid, liquid
and gas.
● The three states of matter are interconvertible.
KINETIC THEORY OF MATTER
●  All forms of matter is composed of small particles (molecules, atoms,
ions).



● The particles are in continuous, never-ending motion (Brownian motion).
The higher the temperature, the faster is the motion, as thermal energy is
converted into kinetic energy.
● The particles attract one another with a force (intermolecular force) which
decreases with the increasing distance between them.
CHARACTERISTICS OF MATTER
●  In a solid, the particles are packed closely together, so, inter-particle
attractive force is very high.
● Inter–particle force is weak in liquids, so they are able to flow and occupy
shape of container.
●  Inter-particle force is negligible in gases. So their particles are able to
wander about more freely than those of liquids or solids.
● Their free random motion is called Brownian motion.
CHANGES IN STATES OF MATTER
●  Heating increases the kinetic energy and consequently motion of the
particles and the rigid structure of the solid is lost.
● Further heating leads to escaping of the particles from the surface.
●  When heat is removed from a gas (vapour) the kinetic energy of the
particles is reduced and they become slower and thus come nearer.
●  This leads to increase in inter-particle attractive forces and thus
condensation and finally freezing takes place.
● Sublimation occurs, where the attractive forces between the particles are
low. It involves change of a solid directly into gas on heating without
undergoing liquid state or vice versa.
CLASSIFICATION OF MATTER
ELEMENTS
●  An element is a pure substance that is composed of only one kind of
atoms.
●  An element cannot be composed from or decomposed into simpler
substance by a physical or chemical change.
Examples : Aluminium (Al), Hydrogen (H), Iron (Fe) etc.
● Metals are characterised by :
● Metallic Lustre
● Good thermal and electrical conductivity
● Ductility (can be drawn into wires) e.g. copper, gold



● Malleability (can be beaten into sheets) e.g.–Iron, Aluminium
● Gold is most ductile and malleable hence used in making ornaments.
● Non-metals are characterised by absence of above properties. e.g. Oxygen,
Chlorine etc.
● Metalloids are elements which exhibit properties midway between those of
metals and non-metals, e.g., Arsenic, Antimony.
● Except Mercury, all metals are solid at ordinary temperatures.
●  Except Bromine, all non-metals are gases or solids under ordinary
conditions.
● Percentage of different elements in human body.
Oxygen – 65%, Carbon – 18 %, Hydrogen – 10 %, Nitrogen – 3 %, others –
4 %
COMPOUNDS
● Compound is a pure substance that is composed of two or more elements
chemically combined in definite and constant proportions. e.g. water (H2O),
Sodium Chloride (NaCl) etc.
● The separation of a compound into its constituent elements by chemical
means is called analysis a part of analytical chemistry.
● The formation of a compound by the union of elements is called synthesis.
●  Silicon dioxide (SiO2) is the most commonly found compound on the
earth crust in lithosphere while oxygen is the most commonly found element
on earth.
● The total number of different elements present in a molecule of compound
is called its atomicity such as its 2 for NaCl and 7 for H2SO4.

MIXTURES :
● A given mixture can be a solution, colloid or suspension. The three are
different in their behaviour as listed below in table.
TYPES OF COLLOIDS

 Dispersed phase Dispersion medium Example

1. Solid Solid Solid sol., minerals and
rocks, gems

2. Solid Liquid Sol, gold sol., sulphur



sol.

4. Liquid Liquid Emulsion, milk
5. Liquid Solid Gel, cheese, jellies
6. Liquid Gas Liquid aerosol, cloud
7. Gas Liquid Foam, froth in air
8. Gas Solid Solid foam, pumic stone

SOLUTIONS, SOLUTE AND SOLVENT
● A solution is a homogenous mixture of two or more substances.
● The substance which is dissolved to make a solution is called solute and
the liquid in which solute is dissolved is known as solvent.
● The true solutions are always homogenous and the size of solute particles

is less than 10–7 cm.
●  In a true solution, the solute particles cannot be distinguished from the
solvent molecules even by using a microscope.
● A colloid is a kind of solution in which the size of solute particles is bigger
than that of a true solution but smaller than those of a suspension.
●  Milk, Blood, Ink, Soap solution, Gum, Jelly are example of colloidal
solutions.
● The scattering of light by colloidal particles is called Tyndall effect.
● Strength of a solution : It is defined as the amount of the solute in grams

found dissolved in one litre (or dm3) of solution. It is expressed in g/litre or

g/dm3.



● Molarity : For a solution, it is defined as number of moles of the solute

dissolved per litre (or dm3) of solution. It is denoted by M:
MIXTURES
●  A mixture is matter composed of two or more substances (elements,
compounds or both) in variable proportions in which each of its components
still exhibit its own characteristics properties.
● Mixtures can be heterogeneous i.e. non uniform, or homogeneous, i.e. the
same throughout.
● Homogeneous mixtures have the same composition throughout the sample
e.g. salt solution.
● Heterogeneous mixture consists of two or more parts which have different
compositions. e.g. mixture of iron and sulphur.
Separation of a pure substance : A substance known for its specific properties
such as boiling point, melting point, density, refractive index, etc is called a
pure substance.
● From a given mixture, a pure substance can be separated depending upon
the property of the substance or of the present impurity.
There are different methods known and adopted to separate a pure substance
from a given mixture such as filtration, crystallization, sedimentation
followed by decantation, distillation provided that the difference between the
two liquids is minimum of 25°C, fractional distillation, chromatography,
sublimation, centrifugation, etc. of these processes:
● evaporation and crystalization is used to obtain salt from sea water.
●  fractional distillation is used to separate different fractions of petroleum
such as gasoline, diesel, kerosene oil etc. from crude oil.
● centrifugation of millk is used to separate cream from raw millk.
●  sublimation is done to separate substance such as camphor which
sublimate on heating.
● filtration is used to separate insoluble substances from a mixture.
●  gravity separation wing separating funnel is used to separate two
immiscible liquids such as oil and water.
● reverse osmosis to separate potable water from marine water.
PROPERTIES OF GASES AND GAS LAWS
Kinetic-Molecular theory of Gases



● Gases consist of molecules widely separated in space. The total volume of
the molecules is negligible compared to the volume of the gas as a whole. It
occupies both the shape and volume of container.
Boyle’s Law
● The volume (V) of a given mass of a dry gas is inversely proportional to
the pressure (P), if the temperature remains constant i.e., or, P1V1 = P2V2
Charle’s Law
● At constant pressure, the volume
(V) of given mass of gas is directly proportional to the absolute (Kelvin)
temperature (T) i.e., V ∝ T.
● Ideal Gas Equation : PV/T = Constant
● Charles law explains that gases expand on heating. Therefore, the mass of
gas sample is constant, the larger volume of gas at high temperature has less
mass per unit volume i.e., lower density.
● Hence, hot air is less dense than cool air. This allows hot air balloons to be
used for meteorological purposes or transportation. They rise up in cool air
or humid air rise up in atmosphere.
Ideal gas :
● A gas that obeys Boyle’s law, Charle’s law and Avogardo’s law strictly is
called an ideal gas.
Combined gas law states that P1V1 = P2V2 or
= Hence, gas equation is PV = nRT where R represents work done per degree
per mol or energy per degree per mole. Numerically it is equal to 0.0821 L

atm K–1m–1.
●  Dalton’s law of partial pressure : It states that the pressure of a
homogenous mixture of gases like air is equal to the sum of the partial
pressure of individual gas present.
P = P1 + P2 + P3 ..... .
● Non-ideal gas : It is also called real gas and does not follow all general gas
equations at all temperatures and pressure.
● Diffusion : It refers to the movement of fluid particles i.e., liquid or gas
from the region of high concentration to the region of low concentration.
● Osmosis is special type of diffusion that involves the movement of solvent
particles only across a membrane from hypotonic solution to by hypertonic



solution.
ATOMIC STRUCTURE
●  An atom consists of a small positively charged heavy nucleus with
negatively charged light electrons revolving around the nucleus in circular
orbits. (Rutherford Atomic Model)
● The nucleus contains protons and neutrons, called nucleons.
● Proton is a positively charged particle having an absolute charge of 1.6

×10-19 coulomb and mass of 1.672 × 10–27kg.
● Electron is a negatively charged particle having an absolute charge of –1.6

× 10–19 coulomb and mass of 9.109 × 10–31kg.

●  Neutron is an uncharged or neutral particle with mass of 1.674 × 10–
27kg.
Symbols of some Important Particles
Particle Symbol Atomic No. Mass No.

Particle Atomic No. Mass No.

Beta(P) particle -1 0

Neutron 0 1
Gamma ray (y) 0 0
Deutron 1 2
Positron 1 0
Neutrino 0 0

FACTS TO REMEMBER
● J.J. Thomson discovered electron in cathode ray discharge tube. The ratio
of charge to mass i.e. e/m is same for all cathode rays irrespective of gas used
in discharge tube.
● Goldstein used the discharge tube and anode rays consisting of proton.
He described them to be located in the nucleus of atom.



●  Presence of newtrons as neutron sup-atomic particle in mucleus was
discovered and proposed by Chadwick.
●  The set of four quantum numbers are Principal quantum number (n),
Azimuthal quantum number (l, l = 0, 1, 2, 3, ... (n –1), Magnetic quantum
number (m, n = 1, 0, + 1...) and Spin quantum number with value +1/2 or –
1/2.
● Nucleus, consisting of protons and neutrons accounts for almost the whole
mass of the atom.
● Extra-nuclear part is occupied by electrons orbiting in fixed energy levels.
● Having equal number of protons and electorns, atom as a whole is neutral.
● Mass number (A) is the number of protons plus the number of neutrons in
the nucleus, also called atomic mass.
●  Atomic number (Z) is a number equal to the number of protons in the
nucleus.
● Electronic configuration describes the manner in which electrons arrange
themselves in the various orbits based on the relation 2n2 where ‘n’ is the
number of shell. However, the last and second last shell cannot have more
than eight electrons.

Mole : It refers to a group of 6.023 × 1023 particles, atoms or molecules of
an element or compound respectively.
No. of moles of element = Mass of element Gram atomic mass of element
No. of moles of compound = Molecular mass in grams Gram atomic mass of
compound Mass of an atom of an element = Gram atomic mass 6.023×1023

SOME PRINCIPLES
●  Heisenberg’s uncertainly principle : It is not possible to determine
simultaneously the position and the momentum of a body to an arbitrary
accuracy.
● Pauli’s exclusion principle : No two electrons in an atom can have the
same set of 4 quantum numbers.
● Hund’s rule : Pairing of electrons in degenerate orbitals belonging to a
particular sub-shell does not take place till each orbital is occupied by a
single electron with parallel spin.
● Aufbau’s principle : In the ground state of an atom, electron enters the
orbital of lowest energy first. Any subsequent electrons are filled in order of



increasing energies. For multi-electron atoms, the sequence observed is 1s,
2s, 2p, 3s, 3p, 4s, 3d, 4p, 5s, 5d ....
ISOTOPES
● Isotopes are atoms of the same element having the same atomic number,
but different atomic weights or mass numbers. e.g. Protium (1H1),
Deuterium (1H2), Tritium (1H3) are isotopes of hydrogen, C12 and C14
are isotopes of carbon.
ISOBARS
● Isobars are atoms of two elements having the same mass number, though
different atomic number, e.g. both Argon (Ar) and Calcium (Ca) have mass
number = 40, C-14 and Nitrogen have mass number = 14 but they have
different atomic number.
ISOTONES
● Isotones are atoms with different atomic number and mass number, but
having same number of neutrons
●  Isoelectronic species : These are chemical species which have same
numbers of electrons such as (O and NO2+ each have sum of 20 electrons.
● Isodiaphers : In nuclear physics, isodiaphers refers to nuclides which have
different atomic numbers and mass numbers but the same number of excess
neutrons i.e., difference in number of neutrons and protons e.g. 90th 234 and
92nd 238, with = 54.
● Isosters : These are molecules or ions with same number of atoms and
same number of atoms and same number of valence electrons e.g. hydrogen
ion and fluoride ions, sodium and hydrogen cations on O2 and N2O.

PERIODIC TABLE OF ELEMENTS
● Periodic table is the arrangement of elements according to some recurring
property.
●  Total number of known elements is 114, of which 92 are naturally
occurring elements and others are artificially prepared in laboratories called
transuranic elements.
●  Mendeleev’s periodic table, based upon Atomic Weights was the first
really successful arrangements of elements.



● Modern periodic table or the extended form or long form of the periodic
table proposed by Moseley is based upon Atomic Number.
Groups
● Vertical columns of the table are called groups.
● Group-number is assigned to an element depending upon the number of
valence electrons (electrons in outer most valence shell) present in the atom
of the element.
●  There are 8 main and 18 vertical columns representing the various sub-
groups, out of these,
● 14 columns represent 14 subgroups of the first seven groups, ● 3 columns
for the elements of group VIII,
● 1 column for the zero group elements,
● Group 1 A: Alkali metals,
● Group II A: Alkaline earth metals,
● Group VII A : Halogens,
● Zero Group: Noble gases,
● All Group B elements: Transition metals, because their chemical properties
are between those of elements in Group II and Group III. These elements
such as mangnese, from copper, etc are known to show variable valency with
vacant d-orbital.
Periods
● Horizontal rows are called periods.
● There are 7 periods in the table, each beginning with an alkali metal on the
left and ending with a noble gas on the right [exception 1st and 7th period].
Ist period has two elements and 7th period has 29 element instead of 32
element.
Periodic Properties
●  Atomic radii is the distance between the centre of nucleus and the
outermost valency shell of the atom. It reduces moving across a period from
left to right i.e., element X in 2nd group of 2nd period has larger radii
compared to element Y in 7th group of the same period.
● Ionisation (energy) potential is the amount of energy required to remove
one or more electrons from the valency shell of an isolated gaseous atom. It
is expressed in KJ mol–1. Always Ist ionisation is less than 2nd ionisation
energy because of increased electrostatic force.



● Electron Affinity is the amount of energy released on adding one or more
electrons to the valence shell of an isolated gaseous atom. I.E1 < I.E2 <
I.E3
● Electronegativity is the tendency of an atom to attract electrons to itself
when combined in a compound. In a period, the electronegativity increases
across a period from left to right while in a group it decreases from top to
bottom. Fluorine is the most electronegative element and Cesium is the most
electropositive among all elements.
● An element is considered as a metal, if it loses electrons when supplied
with energy. When an element gains electrons, it is considered as non–metal.
Metallic character decreases across the period and non-metallic
Characteristic increases. Metal form basic oxides and non-metals form acidic
oxides.
CHEMICAL BONDING
Chemical bond is meant to help atom achieve the next gas electron
arrangement and become stable. An atom achieves this by forming bond with
other atoms of same element or atom belonging to different element.
The compound formed can be electrovalent or covalent that depends on type
of bonding involved incompound formation.
Electrovalent bond all strong bonds fomed between the cation and anion i.e.
atoms that can form cation and anion i.e. atoms that can form cation or anion
can only be involved in electrovalent bonding such as members of alkali
metals and halogens.
Covalent bond is formed between atoms of similar elements or those that do
not undergo ionisation to form cation or anion.
FACTS TO REMEMBER
● Chemical bonds are formed as a result of loss, gain or sharing of electrons
between the atom of reacting elements.
● Atoms tend to combine together to change from an unstable form with an
incomplete outer shell to a more stable form having complete shell.
● 2 electrons are present in the first and outer–most shell, e.g., Helium
● Or, 8 electrons in the outer–most shell, e.g., inert gases other than helium
and hydrogen.
● The linkage which actually holds the atoms together within the molecule is
called a chemical bond.



● Cation is the positively charged particle formed by loss of an electron or

more. e.g. Na+, Mg2+, Ca2+, etc
● Anion is the negatively charged particle formed by gain of an electron or

more. e.g. Cl–, O2–, etc.
● Valence electrons is the number of electrons present in the outermost shell
of an atom.
● Valency of an element is the number of electrons lost, gained or shared by
one atom of the element during the course of chemical bonding.
●  Electropositive nature is the tendency of an atom to lose electrons and
form cations. e.g. Metals like sodium, potassium etc.
● Electro negative nature is the tendency of an atom to gain electrons and
become anion e.g. Non-metals like hydrogen, chlorine etc.,
● Electrovalent or ionic bond is the bond formed by transfer of one or more
electrons, from the atom of an electropositive element to the atom of an
electronegative element, e.g., NaCl, MgCl2,CaO.
● Covalent bond is the chemical bond formed between two combining atoms
by mutual sharing of one or more electrons of atoms of non–metallic
element, e.g. H2O, NH3, H2, N2 AlCl3, etc.
● A bond is polar, if the charge is unevenly spread over the bond like in
water molecule and non-Polar, if charge is evenly spread.
● Coordinate bond is a type of covalent bond in which only one of the atoms
contributes both the electrons of the shared electron pair, e.g. H2SO4,NH3.
●  Hydrogen bond is an electrostatic force between covalently bonded
hydrogen atom of one molecule and an electronegative atom (such as,
Fluorine (F), Oxygen (O), Nitrogen (N) of another molecule)
● Hydrogen bond is responsible for exceptionally high melting and boiling
points of water (H2O), Ammonia (NH3) and Hydrogen fluoride (HF).
● Van der Waal’s bond is the force of attraction between neighbouring atoms
because of their different dipoles induced by instantaneous unsymmetrical
distribution of electrons in the atom. It is responsible for condensation of
inert elements, and gases, like H2,N2,O2, methane etc.
●  Metallic bond is the attractive force between mobile electrons and the
metallic Kernels, e.g. Gold, Silver, Copper etc.



● In isoelectronic series the ions of different elements have same number of
electrons but differ from one another in magnitude of nuclear charge.

Such as N3–, O2–, F–, Na+, Mg2+. These all have 10 electrons.
CHEMICAL REACTION AND CHEMICAL EQUATION
● A chemical change is also termed as a chemical reaction.
● In combination reaction, a single or more new substance is/are formed by
the union of two or more substances.
●  In decomposition reaction, a substance breaks up into smaller, simpler
substances.
●  In displacement reaction, one element displaces another element from a
compound.
1. Precipitation reaction is a reaction in which two compounds in their
aqueous state react to form an insoluble residue, called precipitate.
2. Neutralisation reaction is a reaction in which a acid and base react and
exchange their radicals to form salt and water.
● In redox reactions oxidation and reduction take place simultaneously.
● Oxidation is the process in which a substance loses electrons.
● Oxidation is also described as a process involving addition of oxygen, or
removal of hydrogen.
Such as aerobic respiration, combustion of methane, etc.
● Reduction is the process involving gain of electrons. Such as formation of
ammonia in Haber’s process.
●  Reduction is also identified with removal of oxygen, or addition of
hydrogen.
● Oxidising agent is a substance that brings about oxidation, e.g.; Oxygen,
Chlorine, concentrated Sulphuric acid, concentrated and dilute Nitric acid.
● Reducing agent is a substance that brings about reduction e.g.; Hydrogen,
carbon monoxide, Carbon, Ammonia, Coke, Charcoal, Hydrogen sulphide
and Sulphur oxides.
● Endothermic reaction: Heat is absorbed such as neutralization reactions.
● In a electrochemical cell, oxidation take place at anode and reduction at
cathode.
●  Photochemical reaction : Light is required for such reactions such as
photosynthesis and photography changes.
● Chemiluminiscent reaction :
Electricity is required for chemical reaction.



● Electrochemical reaction : Electricity is produced in chemical reaction.
● Nickel and chromium plating is done to prevent steel articles from rusting.
● Gold and silver plating is done on iron or brass objects to improve their
appearance.
● Electro-refining of metals is a process by which impurities from metals are
extracted by chemical processes and are removed electrolytically to obtain a
highly pure metal. In the process pure metal is taken as cathode and impure
metal as anode immersed in an electrolytic solution.
● Metals generally refined by this process are:
1. Zinc 2. Silver 3. Nickel
4. Lead 5. Copper 6. Aluminium
● Electrometallurgy is the extraction of metals from its ores by electrolysis
such as sodium and aluminium from its ore called bauxite.
● Catalyst : It is a substance that cause a reaction to happen at a faster rate
or at low temperature without itself undergoing any chemical change during
course of reation.
ACIDS, BASES AND SALTS
●  Acids are substances, which produce hydronium ion(H3O+) in their
aqueous solution i.e., they are proton donor.
●  Oxy-acids essentially contain oxygen along with hydrogen and other
elements, e.g. H2SO4, HNO3, H2CO3, ● Bases are substances producing
hydroxyl ions in their aqueous solution i.e., they are proton acceptor.
● Oxides and hydroxides of metals (or metal like radicals) are called bases.
●  Alkali is a water-soluble base, e.g. sodium hydroxide and potassium
hydroxide.
● A weak acid remains weak even if it is concentrated because it produces a
low concentration of hydrogen ions.
●  A strong acid remains a strong acid even if it is diluted, because it
produces a high concentration of hydrogen ions.
● Acids generally have a sour taste, while bases have a bitter taste.
● Strong acids are corrosive and can burn through the skin.
● Bases are soapy to touch.
● Whenever an acid reacts with an alkali or base or vice versa, salt is formed
along with water. Salt formed can be acidic, basic or neutral depending on
nature of acids and bases.



SOME IMPORTANT USES OF ACIDS, BASES AND SALTS
Acids
● Sulphuric acid : King of chemicals is used in manufacture of fertilizers,
chemicals, explosives, paints, dyes, drugs and electrolytic cell like lead
storage battery.
●  Nitric acid : Used in manufacture of fertilizers, chemicals, explosives,
paints, dyes and drugs.
● Hydrochloric acid : Tanning and printing industry.
● Citric acid : In medicine (source of vitamin C), flavouring drinks.
● Boric acid : As an eye-wash, preservation of grains.
● Acetic acid : Flavouring food, food preservation (Vinegar).
● Benzoic acid : Food preservation (sodium benzoate).
● Carbonic acid : It lends ‘fizz’ to aerated drinks called fizzy drinks.
● Oxalic acid : In ink-stain removers.
●  Tartaric acid : Constituent of baking powder (reacts with the sodium
bicarbonate to release carbon dioxide that makes the dough, porous, light and
spongy).
Bases
● Sodium hydroxide : Manufacture of soap.
● Calcium hydroxide :
(i) Manufacture of bleaching powder, mortar, (ii) Softening of hard water,
(iii) Neutralising acid in the soil and in water supplies.
● Potassium hydroxide : Alkaline batteries.
●  Magnesium hydroxide : As an antacid to neutralise acidity caused by
excessive hydrochloric acid secretion in the stomach.
● Aluminium hydroxide : Foaming agent in fire extinguishers and as antacids.
● Ammonium hydroxide : Used to remove grease stains from clothes.
pH value/scale :
pH refers to the concentration of hydrogen ions in a solution as compared to
the hydroxide solution.

Salts
● Salts are ionic compounds containing a positive ion (cation) and a negative
ion (anion).
● A salt formed can be acidic, basic or neutral.
● Sodium chloride (NaCl) is a normal neutral salt.
●  Mohr’s salt [FeSO4(NH4)2, SO4.6H2O] and Alum
[K2SO4.Al2(SO4).3 24H2O] are double salt.
● Hydrolysis is a reaction in which a salt reacts with water to form a solution
which is either acidic or alkaline.
● Efflorescence is the property of salts to lose their water of crystallisation
and hence, are transformed into a powder at ordinary temperatures.
●  Deliquescence is the property of salts to absorb moisture at ordinary
temperatures to lose their crystalline form and form a saturated solution like
NaCl, anhydrous copper sulphate or cobalt chloride.
●  Hygroscopy is the property of salts to absorb atmospheric moisture at
ordinary temperature without dissolving in it.
ELECTROLYSIS
● Electrolysis is a chemical process by which a chemical substance, in its
fused state or in aqueous solutions, is decomposed by the passage of an
electric current, leading to the discharge of ions of the electrolyte at the two
electrodes, cathode and anode.
● Electrolyte is a compound which, in the fused state or in aqueous solution,
allows the passage of an electric current and is decomposed by it.
● Example of strong electrolytes :
1. Strong acids : Hydrochloric acid, nitric acid, sulphuric acid.
2. Strong bases : Sodium hydroxide, potassium hydroxide, barium hydroxide.
3. Salts : Lead bromide, sodium chloride, silver nitrate in aqueous state only.
● Examples of weak electrolytes :
1. Weak Acids : Acetic acid, carbonic acid, formic acid.
2. Weak bases : Ammonium hydroxide, magnesium hydroxide, copper
hydroxide.



3. Salts : Lead acetate, sodium carbonate.
●  Compounds, which in the fused state or in the aqueous solution do not
dissociate into ions are called non-electrolytes.
●  Examples : Distilled water, Acetone, Sugar solution, Petrol, Alcohol,
Benzene, Glycerol.
●  Electrodes are the two conducting poles of a metal or graphite, through
which electric current, i.e., electrons enter or leave an electrolyte in fused or
aqueous solution state.
1. Anode is the electrode connected to the positive terminal of the battery.
The current enters the electrolyte through the anode. At the anode the atom
loses electrons to form neutral cation.
2. Cathode is the electrode connected to the negative terminal of the battery.
The current leaves the electrolyte through the cathode. At the cathode, the
cation gains electrons to form neutral atoms.
● Electrolytic cell or voltameter is the complete set-up or vessel consisting of
electrodes and electrolytes, in which electrolysis takes place.
●  Corrosion : It is natural process that converts a refined metal into its
oxide, sulphide, etc causing the gradual destruction of metal or its gradual
wasting away.
● Steel : It is an alloy of iron having variable amounts of carbon, manganese
and other elements.

Type of steel Carbon Manganese
mild steel 0.05% 0.4%
medium carbon steel 0.30% 1%
High carbon steel 0.5 - 1.0% 0.3 - 0.9%
● Stainless steel : It is an alloy of iron with minimum 10.5 % chromium.
The other include nickel, molybdenum, titanium or copper.
● Prevention of corrosion : Any step adopted to avoid metal surface from
coming in contact of air and moisture prevents corrosin such as aling,
greasing, painting, galvanisation, using zink or electro plating.
Electro chemical cell :
It consist of a system that converts chemical energy into electrical energy. An
electro chemical cell can be primary or secondary. A cell is primary if it
cannot be reacharged like a dry cell of 1.5V but a secondary cell like lead
storage battery can be recharged.



● Microbial fuel cell : (MFC) It’s a device that converts chemical energy to
electrical energy by the action of micro-organisms. These cells use either bio
anode or bio cathode with a membrane to separate the compartments of
cathode and anode. The electrons produced during oxidation are transferred
to electrodes.
METALS AND NON-METALS
FACTS TO REMEMBER
● A metal is an electropositive element which is hard, sonorous, lustrous,
malleable, ductile with tensile strength, and a good conductor of heat and
electricity.
● Metals form basic or amphoteric oxides.
● A non-metal is an electronegative element which occurs as solids, liquids
and gases. They are generally poor conductors of heat and electricity.
● Non-metallic if solid are usually soft and brittle.
● Minerals are solid, naturally occurring as deposits in the earth’s crust, and
contain metals in the combined state, along with associated earthly impurties
(gangue).
● Ores are commercially viable minerals from which metal can be extracted
profitably. Therefore, all ores are minerals but all minerals cannot be ores.
●  Alloy is a homogenous mixture of two or more metals (or metal and
nonmetal) combined in a definite proportion in their molten state.
● Amalgam is an alloy in which one of the components is mercury.
● Mercury and gallium, despite being metals are liquids at room temperature.
● Vermilion is made from the mercury ore, Cinnabar (Mercury-sulphide).
● Gold and platinum occur exclusively in free state.
● Copper, mercury and silver occur partly in free state.
●  Most of the metals occur in the form of oxides, sulphides, carbonates,
sulphates, chlorides and silicates.
● Lithium is the lightest metal.
● Iridium has the highest density among metals.
● Liquation is used to concentrate metals having a low melting point, e.g.,
lead and tin.
●  Distillation is used to concentrate volatile metals like mercury and zinc
which contain non-volatile impurities.
●  Oxidation is used to concentrate metals containing volatile impurities,
which are easily oxidised e.g., Pig iron.



● A carbonate ore is converted into metal oxide by Calcination i.e. heating in
absence of air.
●  A sulphide ore is converted into metal oxide by roasting. i.e. heating in
presence of oxygen.
● The metal oxides are reduced to metal by carbon, aluminium or electrolytic
reduction.
● The refining of metals can be done by electrolysis, liquation, distillation or
oxidation methods.
● Forth Floatation process is used for the concentration of sulphide ores.
LIST OF COMMON ORES
1. Iron : Hematite (Fe2O3), Magnetite (Fe3O4), Ironphyrite (FeS2),
Siderite (FeCO3)
2. Aluminium : Bauxite (Al2O3.2H2O), Corrundum (Al2O3), Kryolite
(Na3AlF6)
3. Copper : Copper pyrite (CuFeS2), Copper glance (Cu2S)
4. Zinc : Zincblende (ZnS), Calamine (ZnCO3)
5. Potassium : Karnalite (KCl MgCl2 6H2O), Salt petre (KNO3)
6. Lead : Galena (PbS), Angelsite (PbCl2)
7. Tin : Tin pyrities (Cu2 FeSnS4), Cassiterite (SnO2)
8. Silver : Silver glance (Ag2S), Ruby Silver (3Ag2 S. Sb2 S3)
9. Gold : Calverite (Au Te2), Silvenites (AgAuTe2)
10. Mercury : Cinnabar (HgS), Caloment (Hg2Cl2)
11. Magnesium : Magnesite (MgCO3), Dolomite (MgCO3 CaCO3),
Epsom salt (MgSO4.7H2O), Kieserite (MgSO4. H2O)

COMPOUNDS OF SODIUM
The important compounds of sodium metal are Common Salt, Washing Soda
and Baking Soda.
(1) Common Salt (NaCl)
●  Common salt is sodium chloride, NaCl. It is a colourless crystalline
substance which is highly soluble in water. It is hygroscopic due to the
presence of small amount of magnesium chloride. It is used in flavouring
food and in food preservation.
(2) Washing Soda (Sodium Carbonate)
● Chemically, washing soda is a carbonate of sodium metal. It is actually
sodium carbonate decahydrate, Na2CO3. 10H2O. It is used to remove
hardness of water and in cleaning of fabrics.
(3) Baking soda (NaHCO3)
● Its chemical name is sodium bicarbonate or sodium hydrogen carbonate. It
is used in water fire extinguisher and as baking powder in cooking. Baking
powder is mixture of sodium bicarbonate and tartaric acid.
Glass
It is super cooled liquid formed from, sand, lime stone, soda ash and cullet
(broken pieces of glass). The mixture is heated in regenerative furnace.
The glass produced is called soda glass. The commercially produced glass is
borosilicate. In borosilicate glass soda is replaced with boron oxide. The
glass commercially used is imparted different colour for different reason.
Bullet Proof Glass
It is the glass that provides the protection against the fire or bullet. It is made
of poly carbonate, thermoplastic and laminated glass.



Glass is also considered as super cooled liquid. It is considered as highly
viscous substance.
COMPOUNDS OF CALCIUM
(1) Lime (Calcium Oxide, CaO)
● Chemically lime is calcium oxide and its formula is CaO. It is also called as
Quick Lime. It is prepared by heating limestone to a temperature of 800°C to
1000°C in a lime kiln.
● Its melting point is very high being 2600°C.
Use of Lime
(1) Calcium oxide (lime) is used in the manufacture of cement and glass.
(2) Calcium oxide (lime) is used for drying gases and alcohol.
(3) Calcium oxide (lime) is used as a basic lining in furnaces.
(4) Calcium oxide (lime) is used in the preparation of calcium carbide, basic
calcium nitrate, and calcium bisulphite.
(5) It is used in slaking of lime i.e. formation of calcium hydroxide.
Uses of Calcium Hydroxide (Slaked Lime)
(1) A solution of calcium hydroxide (slaked lime) in water called lime-water
is used for testing carbon dioxide and sulphur dioxide gas in chemistry
laboratories. With carbon dioxide white ppt. formation is temporary but
permanent with sulphur dioxide.
● Hard and soft water : Water obtained from natural water reservoirs such
as wells, bore holes, rivers etc. is hardwater being rich in the chloride and
sulphate salts of calcium and magnesium. Although good for health, it’s not
good for laundry purposes. It does not form lather with soap and when boiled
leaves scale of salt deposits. It is denser than soft water.
(2) Bleaching Powder
● Chemically, bleaching powder is calcium oxychloride.
●  Its formula is CaOCl2, Ca(OH)2 + Cl2 → CaOCl2 + H2O It is
produced by passing chlorine gas over dry slaked lime. It is soluble in cold
water. It is a yellowish white powder which gives a strong smell of chlorine if
left exposed to air.
Uses of Bleaching Powder
(1) Bleaching powder is commonly used for bleaching washed clothes in
laundry (laundry is a place where clothes are washed and pressed).
(2) Bleaching powder is used for bleaching cotton and linen in textile
industry and for bleaching wood pulp in paper industry.



(3) Bleaching powder is used for disinfecting drinking water supply, i.e. for
making drinking water free from germs (chlorination).
(4) Bleaching powder is used for the manufacture of chloroform (CHCl3).
(5) Bleaching powder is used for making wool unshrinkable.
(6) Bleaching powder is used as an oxidising agent in many chemical
industries.
(3) Plaster of Paris
● Chemically it is calcium sulphate hemihydrate.
● Its formula is (CaSO4)2. H2O. or CaSO4 1/2 .H2O It is prepared from
gypsum.
● Gypsum is calcium sulphate dihydrate, CaSO4. 2H2O.
● When Gypsum is heated to a temperature of 100°C, it loses threefourths of
its water of crystallisation and forms Plaster of Paris. If heated at higher
temperature, it produces dead plaster.
● Plaster of Paris is a white powder. It sets into a hard mass on wetting with
water.
Uses of Plaster of Paris
(1) Plaster of Paris is used in hospitals for setting fractured bones in the right
position to ensure correct healing. It keeps the fractured bone straight. This
use is based on the fact that when Plaster of Paris is mixed with water and
applied around the fractured limbs, it sets into a hard mass. In this way, it
keeps the bone joints or fractured bone in a fixed position.
(2) Plaster of Paris is used in chemistry laboratories for sealing the airgaps in
apparatus where air-tight arrangement is required.
(3) Plaster of Paris is used in making castings for statues, cheap ornaments,
toys and decorative materials.
(4) Plaster of Paris is used as a fire-proofing material.
(5) Plaster of Paris is used in making black-board chalk.
Allotropy
It is the property in which an element exhibit itself in two or more than two
physical forms like carbon, sulphur and phosphorous. Carbon exists in
allotropic form of graphite, diamond, carbon black, charcoal i.e., wood/
animal charcoal and fullerenes with minimum of C-60. Phosphorous exists in
form of white and red phosphorous. Stored in water because of its high
reactivity and low ignition temperature. It is used in match box industry in
making match sticks. Wood charcoal is used as fuel for absorption of gases in



gas masks. Animal charcoal is used to remove pigment during filtration of
molasses in the sugar industries.
ELEMENTS AND THEIR OCCURRENCE IN EARTH
●  Earth is a rich source of elements, found either in native state or in
compound form.
● The solid phase of the earth is called the lithosphere.
● Percentage distribution of elements in earth’s crust is as shown below :
● Ocean and seas, which form major component of hydrosphere, are again
vast reservoirs of elements.
● The major elements are Chlorine, Sodium, Magnesium, Sulphur, Calcium,
Potassium and Carbon in that order.
●  The minor elements are Bromine, Boron, Strontium, Silicon, Nitrogen,
Lithium, Aluminium and Rubidium.
● Four elements, Chlorine, Bromine, Sodium and Magnesium are recovered
commercially from sea water.
● The Manganese nodules found in the sea bed may form a potential source
of some of the less common metals.
● The gaseous phase of the earth is called atmosphere.
● The atmosphere mainly consists of nitrogen (78.09%), Oxygen (20.95%).
Argon (0.93) and other gases (<1%). These gases are obtained by fractional
distillation of air.
● Carbon dioxide forms about 0.04% of the atmosphere.
● Ozone exists mostly at the outer atmospheric level, where it helps in the
absorption of solar ultraviolet radiation. Therefore, ozone is considered as
chemical weed. On earth surface it is bluish toxic gas but in stratosphere, it is
useful in protecting earth from UV-rays.
● Examples of elements accumulated in living organisms are :
1. Iodine in sea weeds
2. Vanadium in sea cucumbers
3. Potassium in plant leaf
● Examples of elements concentrated in different parts of living beings are :
1. Iron (Fe-11) in blood
2. Zinc in the eyes of certain animals
3. Magnesium in chloroplast
4. Copper in mollscus have blue colour fluid (blood) because copperions.
FUELS



●  The materials which on combustion produce heat and light energy are
known as fuels.
● On the basis of their physical state fuels can be classified into solid fuels,
liquid fuels and gaseous fuels.
(i) Solid fuels : Coal, coke, wood, charcoal, nuclear fuels.
(ii) Liquid fuels : Petrol (Gasoline), diesel, kerosene, alcohol.
(iii) Gaseous fuels : Natural gas, coal gas, LPG, water gas, gobar gas
(Biogas), producer gas.
● The waste material of living objects like cattle dung and the dead parts of
living objects like plants, trees, is called Bio–mass.
●  Bio-mass contains carbon compounds and is the oldest source of heat
energy for domestic purposes.
●  Examles of Bio-mass being used as a fuel are, wood, cattle dung and
agricultural wastes like bagasse.
●  The destructive distillation of wood yields wood gas, charcoal, methyl
alcohol, acetic acid.
●  Bio-gas is a mixture of methane (65%), carbon-dioxide, hydrogen and
hydrogen sulphide.
● The major constituent of bio-gas is methane (65%).
● Bio-gas is obtained by the anaerobic fermentation of animal and vegetable
i.e., plant wastes in the absence of water. The decomposition is brought up by
methanogens, bacteria present in cow dung.
●  Khadi and Village Industries Commission (KUIC) is the major
organisation engaged in the promotion and construction of bio-gas plants.
● Coal is a complex mixture of compounds of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen.
●  Coal was formed by the carbonisation of large plants and trees buried
under the earth millions of year ago.
● Bituminous coal is used as fuel in our households, and in industry.
Type of Coal Carbon

Type of Coal Content Carbon
Peat 60%
Lignite 70%
Bituminous 80%
Anthracite 90%



● The destructive distillation of coal yields coal gas, ammoniacal liquor, coal
tar and coke.
● Coke is used as fuel with high calorific value and as the reducing agent in
reduction of metallic oxides to metal.
● Coal gas is a mixture of hydrogen, methane, and carbon monoxide.
● Water gas is a mixture of carbon monoxide and hydrogen.
● Producer gas is a mixture of carbon monoxide and nitrogen.
●  Crude oil was formed by the carbonisation of the remains of extremely
small plants and animals along with procaryotes like bacteria buried under
the sea millions of years ago in high temperature and pressure conditions.
● The refining of petroleum is done by the process of fractional distillation.
● D.D.T., B.H.C., Ethyl Alcohol, Benzene are important petrochemicals.
● Petroleum gas is a mixture of butane, propane and ethane.
● Petroleum gas liquefied under pressure is called Liquefied Petroleum Gas
(LPG).
● LPG consists mainly of butane and small amount of propane and ethane.
● Ethyl mercaptan is added to LPG to help in the detection of gas leakage.
●  Natural gas mainly consists of methane (CH4) and small quantities of
ethane and propane.
●  The process of breaking bigger hydrocarbon molecules into smaller
hydrocarbon molecules by heating in the presence of a catalyst is called
cracking and in absence of oxygen is called pyrolysis.
●  The amount of heat produced by burning a unit mass of the fuel
completely i.e., complete combustion of a gram of fuel is known as its
calorific value.
NOBLE GASES
● There are six noble gases : Helium, Neon, Argon, Krypton, Xenon and
Radon.
● Also called ‘rare gases’, as they are present in very small quantities in the
air.
● Radon is not present in air, it is the rarest of gases.
● Also called ‘inert gases’, as they do not enter into chemical reactions.
●  Helium is used for filling meteorological balloons and also used by sea
divers.
●  Neon is used in making advertising signs. It gives orange-red colour to
fluorescent tubes.

● Argon is used to create an inert atmosphere in chemical reactions and in
electric bulbs. It gives bluishgreen colour to flouorescent tubes.
● Krypton and Xenon are used in electrical valves and TV tubes and also in
lighthouses and miner’s lamp.
●  Xenon is know to react with fluorine or oxygen to form its respective
compound because of their very high electro negative and electropositive
nature.
CARBON AND COMPOUNDS
Carbon is the first element of the 14th group of periodic table with atomic
number six and valency four.
Carbon of its small size and tetravalency has strong tendency to form bonds
with other elements as well as with other carbon atom to form long chain
compounds. The property of carbon to form long straight or branched chain
compounds is called Catenation. Saturated hydrocarbons (Alkane)
Unsaturated hydrocarbons (Depending on number of unsatisfied valencies)
Hydrocarbons Alkene Alkyne
Saturated and Unsaturated hydrocarbons
Saturated hydrocarbons Unsaturated hydrocarbons
1. All the valancies of carbon are All the valancies of carbon are fully
satisfied therefore have not fully satisfied therefore have single covalent
bond. either double or triple covalent bond.
2. They burn with non-sooty flame. They burn with sooty flame.
3. They either do not react or if They react fastly with the bromine react then
very slowly with water to decolourize it.
bromine water to decolourize it.
The hydrocarbons based on valencies of carbon satisfied or not have
representative structural formula depending on number of carbon atoms



(n) present Alkene : CnH2n+2 e.g. CH4, C2H6, C4H10, etc.
Alkene : CnH2n e.g. C2H4, C4H8, C3H6, etc.
Alkyne : CnH2n–2 e.g. C2H2, C3H4, C4H6, etc.
Homologues series : It is a series of hydrocarbon in which all members
(called homologues) follow the same structural formula and every
succeeding differ from the adjacent by –CH2 group or 14 amu by mass. If
any functional group is present then all members have same functional
group.
Isomerism : It is a phenomenon in which a hydrocarbon exists having two or
more structural formula without undergoing any change in its molecular
formula.
When carbon bonds with hydrogen, it form the compounds called
hydrocarbons. The study of hydro carbons or their derivatives is called
organic chemistry.
Denaturation of alcohol : The alcohol consumed by people is ethanol while
methanol also called spirit if consumed leads to blindness and even death.
The ethanol is widely used organic solvent in industries. Hence, to prevent its
consumption by the workers at work, the alcohol used is denatured i.e. made
unfit for human consumption with addition of copper sulphate or pyridine. It
is a heavy, colourless, oily, explosive liquid produced as given above by
nitrating glycerol with white fuming nitric acid under controlled conditions.
TNT : Trinitro toulene (2, 4, 6-trinitro toulene), yellow colour solid with
explosive properties for use in military, industrial and mining operations.
On detonation, it decomposes as:
Dynamite : It is an explosive made of nitroglycerin. Alfred Nobel has
developed dynamite with the use of nitroglycerine as a blasting explosive by
mixing nitroglycerin with inert absorbent particularly, ‘ Icieslguhr’ or
diatomaceous earth and named the mixture dynamite.
RDX : It stands for Research Development Explosive. It is colourless soild
obtained by reacting white fuming nitric acid with hexamine.
Hexamine + Nitric acid RDX + dinitromethene + Ammonium nitrate +
water.
Uses of known organic compounds
1. Ethyl alcohol used as an organic solvent and in the alcohol thermometer as
thermometric fluid to measure low temperature.



2. In alcoholic drinks whisky – 35%, or more wine – 10 – 14%, beer – 4-6%
of ethyl alcohol.
3. Ethyl alcohol is used as an antifreeze in radiators of motor vehicles in cold
countries.
4. Formaldehyde (Methanal) in form of dilute solution is used as preservative
for Biological specimens.
5. Propanone (acetone) is used as nail polish remover, as a solvent and in
manufacture of chloroform and artificial leather and plastic called perspex.
6. Dilute solution of acetic acid (Ethanoic acid) 5% – 7% is vinegar used in
food preservation.
7. Acetic acid is also used in making of artificial fibre cellulose acetate and in
coagulation of rubber from latex obtained from rubber tree.
COMMONLY USED CHEMICALS AT HOUSEHOLD
● Iodized salt : Table salt with a percentage of iodine to prevent goitre.
● Shampoo : Hair care product used for removal of oils, dirt, skin particles,
dandruff and environmental pollutants.
● Soap : It is sodium or potassium salt of long chain fatty acids. It is prepared
by heating equal volume of fatty acid, NaOH and ethanol in water bath until
the paste formation.
● As mixture cools, saturated NaCl solution is added to remove glycerol and
NaOH (Sodium hydroxide).
● Detergent : A detergent is the sodium salt of sulphonic acid.
Soap forms lather only with soft water but detergent is effective in forming
rich lather of foam both in soft water and hard water.
● However, the use of detergents cause water pollution but not of soap.
●  Antiseptics : These are chemical used on living tissues and cells hence
commonly used as hand wash and in treatment of mouth and throat infection
with antibacterial and antifungal properties.
● Disinfectants : There are chemicals that cannot be used on living tissue or
cells. These can be oxidising or non-oxidising disinfectant.
● Oxidizing disinfectants include hydrogen peroxide and hypochlorite, non-
oxidising disinfectants include quaternary ammonium compound, fungicides
and amphoterics.
● Fire extinguishers : There are five types of them used for different fires A,
B, C, D and K.
A-type — Wood, paper, cloth and other ordinary materials.



B-type — Gasoline, oil, paints, etc.
C-type — Electrical fires D-type — Combustible metals.
K-type — Cooking media/ vegetable or animal oils or fats.

Polymerisation
It is the process of reacting monomer molecules together to form polymer
chain. A three dimensional network based on type of plastic formed by
polymerisation is thermoplastic or thermosetting plastic. Thermo plastics are
those that can be remoulded on heating like polyethylene, PVC (Polyvinyl
chloride), poly propylene, PET, etc. Thermo setting plastics are those which
cannot be remoulded after their heating such as bakelite, melanine, urea
formaldehyde etc.
Rubber
Natural rubber not effective for commercial use. It is polymer of isoprene (2-
methyl-1, 3-butadiene) extracted from latex collected from rubber tree.



For commercial use natural rubber is heated with sulphur in process called
the vulcanization to obtain vulcanized rubber. If isoprene is heated with
chlorine the result is chloroprene, fire resistant rubber. The other types of
rubber in use include :
● Styrene butadiene rubber (SRB) used for covering different hoses.
● Butadiene rubber (BR) mostly mixed with other rubber types to improve
elasticity, wear resistance and low temperature properties.
● Neoprene polymer of chloroprene which is fire resistant with high tensile
strength.

###BIOLOGY###

Biology is considered as a natural science concerned with the study of life
and living organism including their structure, function, growth, evolution,
distribution and environment. In general, it recognizes the cell as the basic
unit of life, genes as basic unit of heredity and evolution as the engine that
propels the creation of new species.
BIOLOGICAL CLASSIFICATION
●  For easy understanding of biodiversity, earlier Greek Philosophers like
Aristotle, and his student Theophrastus have proposed two kingdom system.
In 1866 Ernst Heackel proposed three kingdom system of plantae, Animalia
and Protista. As protista kingdom consisted of both prokaryotes and
eucarytoes, Copeland in 1956 proposed four kingdom system.
Four Kingdom System (Monera Protista Planatae Animalia)
● Finally R. Whittaker (1969) using three criteria together i.e. complexity of
cell structure, body structure and mode of nutrition proposed five kingdom
system in which he separated out kingdom fungi from plantae.
Five Kingdom System (Monera Protista Fungi Plantae Animalia)
●  Biological system of taxonomy and systematics got a big boost when
system of ‘Binomial nomenclature, introduced by Carlous Linneaus also
called Father of taxonomy was included into naming of living organism.
According to system, the name were to be written in Latin language and a
name consists of two, generic name to be written first starting with capital
letter followed by specific epithet i.e. name of species. For example, a
population of lions represents the species Panthera leo



CYTOLOGY
A cell first observed by Robert Hooke in 1665 and later by Von
Leeuwenhoek has been described as the structural and functional unit of life.
It is also considered as hereditary unit with genes on DNA in chromosome.
Eukaryote – Within the cell, in most of plants and animals, there are various
membrane bound structures called cell organelles like mitochondria,
chloroplast, nucleus, etc. Such a cell is known as an eukaryote. There are
non-membrane bound organelles also present in eukaryotic cells such as
ribosomes and nucleolus. Each cell organelle performs a specific function of
the cell in a well organised manner.
Prokaryote – In bacteria, mycoplasma, and blue green algae cells, there are
no definite cell organelles and the nucleoplasm and cytoplasm is not
separated i.e. well defined nucleus is lacking in them. Such a cell is known as
a prokaryote. Prokaryotic cells can also carry out all biological functions
similar to those of eukaryotic cells. However, the absence of membrane
bound structures within the cell simply shows that they are primitive cells.

Difference between Plant cell and Animal cell
Plant Cell Animal Cell

Plant cells are larger in size. Animal cells are generally smaller
in size.

Plastid present. Plastid absent.
Cell wall present, made up of cellulose Cell wall absent.
and chitin.
Centro some absent. Centrosome present.
Vacuoles are larger in size. Vacuoles are smaller in size.

 RNA DNA
