(iv) It is found both in the nucleus in organelles like mitochondria and
and in the cytoplasm. chloroplasts.

Cell Division – It is a mode of reproduction in some lower animals like
unicellular organisms like amoeba. In higher organisms i.e. plants and
animals, responsible for growth, repair or replacement of injured and
damaged body parts. In sexually reproducing organisms, it is also involved in
gamete formation. Hence, there are two distinct modes of cell division in
higher organisms called mitosis and meiosis.
Mitosis is an equational cell division responsible for providing growth to an
organism. The process completes in two phases called karyokinesis (nuclear
division) followed by cytokinesis (cytoplasm division).
Meiosis : It is the reductional division restricted to germinal cells involved in
formation of germ cells also called gametes. Thus daughter cells carry half
the number (haploid) chromosomes compared to the number of



chromosomes present in the parental cell. The whole meiosis completes into
two broad phases called meiosis-I and meiosis-II.
BOTANY
● Botany is the scientific study of plants.
Classification of Plants
A. Cryptogamae – These plants do not bear flowers or seeds and reproduce
by spores. They have three divisions.
1. Thallophyte – They are most primitive plants devoid of any body
differentiation.
They are divided into two classes :
(a) Algae – They are simple plants, largely found in marine or freshwater
habitats. Some algae are terrestrial also. e.g. Blue green algae (Nostoc), green
algae (Ulothrix), red algae.
(b) Lichens – Lichens grow on rocks, tree-trunks and are symbiotic
associations between algae and fungi, e.g. Usnea, Parmelia. Lichens are
considered as the pioneer community for succession of plants on rocks
surfaces. They are also considered as the air pollution indicator particularly
one caused by sulphur dioxide. In presence of gas SO2, they die or perish,
hence indicator of air pollution.
2. Bryophyte – These are simple, terrestrial plants commonly found in moist
habitats. e.g. Funaria, Riccia.
3. Pteridophyte – These are vascular non-seed bearing plants. They are
found mainly in shady or damp places.
B. Phanerogamae – These are higher plants bearing flowers and seeds.
There body is differentiated into root, stem and leaves and vascular system is
well–developed. These are divided into Gymnosperms and Angiosperms.
1. Gymnosperms – These lack vessels and are the naked seed bearing
vascular plants. They include cycads and conifers.
2. Angiosperms – They are vascular seed bearing plants characterised by
their flowers containing the male and female reproductive structures.
These are further divided into two groups :
(a) Monocotyledons – They are characterised by parallel leaf venation,
fibrous roots and a single cotyledon in the seed. e.g. wheat (Triticum
vulgare), rice (Oryza Sativa), Maize (Zea mays).
(b) Dicotyledons – They are characterised by reticulate leaf venation, tap
root and having two cotyledons in the seed e.g. Rose (Rose indica), apple



(Malus silvestris), Pea (Pisum sativum).
PLANT TISSUES
On the basis of dividing capacity plant tissues can be classified as :
1. Meristematic tissue
Growth of plants occurs only in regions where meristematic tissues are
present. Depending on the region where they are present, they are classified
as apical, lateral and intercalary meristem.
(a) Apical meristem – It is present at the growing tips of stem and root and
increases the length of the stem and root.
(b) Lateral meristem– It increases the girth of stem or root. It is also called
cambium.
(c) Intercalary meristem– It is located at the base of leaves or internodes on
twigs.
2.Permanent tissue
The cells formed by meristematic tissue acquire permanent shape, size and
function and lose the ability to divide.
A.Simple permanent tissue –
(i) Parenchyma – It consists of unspecialised cell with thin cell walls. The
cells are loosely packed with large intercellular spaces. When it contains
chlorophyll and performs photosynthesis it is called chlorenchyma. In
aquatic plants, they have large air cavities and give buoyancy to the plants
called aerenchyma.
Functions– (i) It provides support to the plants (ii) It stores food and water in
stems and roots.
(ii) Collenchyma– The cells are living, elongated and irregularly thickened
at the corners. They have very little intercellular spaces.
Functions– (i) It provides flexibility to plant parts like leaf and stem. (ii) It
provides mechanical support.
(iii) Sclerenchyma– The cells are dead. They are long and narrow. Walls are
thickened due to deposition of lignin.
Functions – (i) It makes plant hard and stiff., eg. Coconut husk. (ii) It
provides mechanical strength.
Various types of simple tissues (a) parenchyma, (b) collenchyma and (c)
sclerenchyma as shown below :
B.Complex permanent tissue– It is made of more than one type of cells. All
cells perform same function. It is of two types – xylem and phloem.



Xylem– It consists of tracheids, vessels, xylem parenchyma and xylem
fibres. Cell are thick walled and mostly dead.
Functions : (i) Tracheids and vessels transport water and minerals vertically.
(ii) Fibres are supportive.
Phloem
It is made of sieve tubes, companion cells, phloem fibres, and phloem
parenchyma. Except phloem fibres, phloem cells are living.
Function– (i) Transports food from leaves to other parts of the plant.
Various type of complex tissues : (a) Xylem (i) Tracheid (ii) Vessel (b)
Phloem
PLANT STRUCTURE AND FUNCTIONS
The Root
● The plant structure that grows into the soil and anchors the plant to the
ground for absorption of water and minerals.
● Roots develop from the radicle of the embryo.
● Roots differ from shoots in lacking chlorophyll and in the arrangement of
Xylem and Phloem.
Kinds of Root Systems
(1) Tap Root System – Root develops from the radicle and continues
growing actively producing lateral branches. Tap roots are characteristic of
most of the dicot plants.
(2) Fibrous Root System – In monocot the radicle (embryonic root) at the
time of germination produce fibrous roots with many branches.
(3) Adventious Root System – Root develops from any part of the plant
other than the radicle. They occur in grasses and may be regarded as
characteristic of monocot plants.
Modifications of Roots
(1) Fusiform – Swollen root, tapering at both ends. e.g. Sweet potato.
(2) Napiform – Root is globular and tapers abruptly e.g. Beet root, Turnip.
(3) Tuberous – Root with no definite shape e.g. 4’O clock plant.
(4) Fasciculated – When storage roots occur in cluster e.g Dahila,
Asparagus.
(5) Beaded – When roots are swollen at frequent intervals e.g. Bitter gourd.
(6) Assimilatory – Roots which develop chlorophyll and carry out
photosynthesis e.g. Tinospora and Trapa.



(7) Haustoria – These are the roots of the parasitic plants which penetrate
the host tissue e.g. Cuscuta.
(8) Pneumatophores – These are aerial roots found in plants growing in
marshy conditions for gaseous exchange e.g. Rhizophora.
Functions of Root
(1) The roots absorb water and minerals from the soil and transport them
through xylem.
(2) The roots fix the plant to the soil firmly.
(3) Some roots help in vegetative propagation of species e.g. Sweet pptato.
The Stem
●  Stem is a longitudinal axis upon which are borne the leaves, buds and
reproductive organs (flowers) of the plants.
● The stem along with its leafy branches and reproductive appendages called
flowers constitutes the shoot system of the plant.
Modifications of Stems
(1) Underground Modifications –
They are of four types.
(i) Rhizome – horizontally growing, fleshy, underground stem e.g. Ginger,
Turmeric etc.
(ii) Bulb – The stem is reduced to a disc like structure, bearing concentric
layers of fleshy leafy bases that comprises the food store e.g. Onion, Garlic.
(iii) Tuber – A swollen underground stem that contains stored food, and acts
as an organ of perennation and vegetative propagation. e.g. Potato.
(iv) Corm – Short erect fleshy swollen underground stem. e.g. Alocasia,
Colocasia.
The Leaf
●  Leaf is a flattened appendage of the stem that arises as a superficial
outgrowth from the apical meristem.
● Leaves are the chief photosynthetic organs of the plant.
● Collectively leaves constitute the foliage of the plant.
● Leaves initiate from the shoot meristem as Primordia and gradually emerge
as they grow older.
Types of Leaves
● Depending upon the incision of lamina, they are of two kinds —
(i) Simple leaves (ii) Compound leaves.
● Simple Leaves – A leaf is said to be simple when its lamina is entire or is
incised but the incision do not touch the midrib.



● Compound leaves – A leaf is said to be compound when its lamina is
completely divided into two or more distinct segments or leaflets.
● Compound leaves may be of two types (a) Pinnate and (b) Palmate.
The Flower
● Flower is the characteristic reproductive structure of an angiosperm.
●  Morphologically, the flower is considered as a shoot bearing nodes and
modified floral leaves.
● Flowers exhibit wide variation in size, shape, colour and arrangement of
floral parts. Petal
Structure of a flower
Basic Plan of Flowers
● The stalk of the flower is called Pedicel.
● Sepals are green, leaf-like structure that arise at the base of a flower and
forms the outermost circle of appendage.
● Collectively the Sepals are referred to as Calyx. They provide protection to
flower during bud stage.
● The whorl of appendages that arise inner to sepals is petals.
● Petals are brightly coloured and are collectively called Corolla.
● The third group of appendages consists of Stamens, collectively called the
Androecium.
● Each stamen consists of a slender stalk, the filament and the pollen grains
bearing anther.
● The stamens are the male reproductive organs of the flower.
● The centre of the flower contains the female reproductive whorl called the
Gynoecium or Pistil.
● Pistil is composed of one or more carpels.
● Each Carpel consists of three parts — Stigma, Style and Ovary.
Pollination
● The term pollination refers to the transfer and deposition of pollen grains
on the stigmatic surface of the flower. It can be self or cross pollination. Each
flower is well adapted and modified for its mode of pollination.
● In insect pollinated flowers the pollen grains are smooth and sticky but in
wind pollinated flowers, the pollen grains are light, dry and rough. All
sexually reproducing plants are characterized by the double fertilization in
which ovule develops into seed and ovary with its parts develops into fruits.
In some plants like grapes and banana, the fruit development may take place
without fertilization. Such fruits are called parthenocarpic fruits. In some



other plants the cells of other parts of ovary get involved in embryo
formation. Such plants like orange are known to show polyembryoeny.
●  A seed endospermic or not upon receiving water, oxygen from air and
suitable temperature except in some seeds sun light (called photoblastic)
germinate to produce seedling depending upon the transent stored food in
form of endosperm (endosperm) or cotyledon (non-endospermic).
Plant Hormones and their functions

 Plant Hormones and their functions

Hormone Functions
Auxins Prevent premature fall of leaves, fruits etc., promotes

enlargement, stimulates respiration lat crol root
developement.

Gibberellins Promote germination, promote flowering, responsible for
breaking dormancy of buds.

Ethylene Gasous hormone triggers fruit ripening, accelerates
abscission of leaves, flowers and fruits.

Cytokinin Break dormancy in seeds, delay the ageing process in plants.
Abscisic Acid It is a growth regulator, involves in the dormancy of seeds,

acts as a stress hormone.

FRUITS
● A Fruit is a ripened ovary.
●  Fruit is formed through the processes of pollination followed by
fertilization which stimulates the ovary to grow into a fruit.
● The fruit consists of a fruit wall, the Pericarp.
Zones of Fruit Wall (Pericarp)
1. Epicarp – Outer Skin.
2. Mesocarp – Sweet, edible fleshy.
3. Endocarp – Innermost hard zone that encloses the seed(s).
Types of Fruits
1. True Fruits – Fruit which is derived only from the single ovary of a single
flower and in the development of which no other part outside the ovary has
taken part is called the true fruit e.g. Mango.



2. False Fruit – When apart from the ovary other floral parts also take part in
the formation of fruit, it is called as false fruit. e.g. Apple, Banana and
Strawberry.
3. Simple Fruits – A simple fruit is one in which ovary takes part in
development e.g. Bean, Mustard, Citrus.
4. Aggregate Fruit – In an aggregate fruit each free carpel develops
independently to form a bunch of fruits e.g. Strawberry, Custard apple.
5. Composite Fruit – A composite or multiple fruit develops from an
inflorescence by the fusion of flowers and their parts e.g. Pineapple.
Common fruit with types and edible parts

Fruit Type Edible part
Apple Fleshy, Simple, Pome False fruit, fleshy thalamus
Orange Fleshy, Simple, Hesperdium Mesocarp and endocarp

ZOOLOGY
● Zoology is the scientific study of animals.
● The animal kingdom is divided into two main sub-kingdoms – Protozoa
and Metazoa.
● Protozoa are unicellular or acellular organisms. Metazoa are multicellular
organisms.
●  The two major groups of Metazoans are the non-chordates and the
chordates.
● They derive the names from the absence or presence of notochord.
● A notochord is present at same time in the life of Chordates.
● Non-Chordates don’t possess a notochord at any stage of their life.
● Hemi chordates or cephalo chordates have notochord in embryonic stage.



ANIMAL TISSUES
The tissues which make up different parts of an organ of an organism
(animals) body are called animal tissues. The different types of animal
tissues include epithelial tissue, connective tissues, muscular tissues and
nervous tissue.
Epithelial tissues : These are fundamental animal tissues, simple or
stratified with basal membrane called basement membrane. Epithelium can
be endothelium like those involved in formation of the lining of heart, blood
vessels or mesothelium like pleural or pericardial cavities.
Connective tissues : As the name suggests, the tissue is concerned with
connecting of different body parts. There are three types of animal
connective tissue called vascular, connective proper and skeletal tissue.
Blood and lymph are fluid connective tissues. Blood has all three types of
corpuscles i.e., RBC, WBC and blood platelets but lymph has only WBC
being devoid of RBC and blood platelets.
Cartilage and bones are the two types of skeletal connective tissues, cartilage
are soft like outer, ear/nose, tongue etc. with protein called chondrin but
bones are hard deposit of tricalcium phosphate (Ca3(PO4)2). In mouth each
tooth that represents a bone has the enamel, the hardest body part as the
outermost covering being resistant to mechanical or chemical effect. The
mast cells present in areolar tissue produce histamine for allergic action.
Muscular tissues : There are three different types of muscular tissues called
skeletal, smooth and cardiac muscles. The skeletal muscles also called
striated muscles with alternate light and dark bands of protein actin and
myosin are cylindrical, multinucleated with nucleus at periphery and
voluntary. They are mostly found attached to body bones for movement and
locomotion.
● Smooth muscles are involuntary, spindle shape with actin and myosin.
They mostly form the lining of alimentary canal, respiratory tract, etc.
Their alternate contraction and relaxation provides movement to the
substances present in body cavity such as of food in alimentary called
peristalsis.
The Cardiac muscles are striated muscles like skeletal muscles with alternate
light and dark band but are involuntary in action and uninucleated with single
nucleus present in the centre. They are also highly branched and found only
in the heart wall.



●  The whole of nervous tissue is made of nerve cells called neurons, the
longest of body cells and never divides in life once formed in body.
The flow of nerve impulse generated and propagated by Na+
●  In CNS, spinal cord is responsible for coordination of short lived,
involuntary action called reflex action but brain is for voluntary with main
actions such as thinking, memory, reasoning, hearing, vision and balance.
ELEMENTARY PHYSIOLOGY
Human Body
● The Human Body has nine systems :
1. The Digestive System 2. The Respiratory System
3. The Circulatory System 4. The Excretory System 5. The Endocrine
System 6. The Reproductive System.
7. The Skeletal System 8. The Muscular System
9. The Nervous System
The Digestive System
●  Digestion is the process of biochemical transformation of complex and
large food particles into simpler form, making it suitable for absorption and
assimilation. The process of the breakdown of large food molecules into
simpler compounds is catalysed by special enzymes.
Organs involved and their functions
● The Mouth : Ingestion and chewing takes place in the mouth with the help
of teeth. Humans are heterodonts with four types of teeth in pattern of 2 :1 : 2
: 3. A starch hydrolysing enzyme, Ptyalin is present in the saliva added to
food during chewing.
●  The Oesophagus : The mouth leads to a funnel-shaped pharynx which
communicates with a long muscular tube-like oesophagus. The oesophagus
opens into the stomach carrying the food by peristalsis.
● The Stomach : The stomach is a large muscular sac. It has many glands on
its wall. The cells of these gastric glands secrete HCI, proteindigesting
enzymes and mucin in the lumen (cavity) of the stomach. The mixture of
their secretions in the gastric lumen is called Gastric Juice.
It digests proteins in the stomach into amino acids, peptones or peptides.
●  The Small Intestine : The small intenstine is distinguished into three
parts, viz, Duodenum, Jejunum and Ileum. The common bile duct opens into
duodenum and drains juices from pancreas and liver. The jejunum follows
duodenum and is longer and more coiled. The last part or ileum is also



highly–coiled and opens into the large intenstine. Nearly all absorption of
digested material takes place from the wall of the ileum through the villi.
From intestine absorbed food is taken to liver by hepatic portal vein.
● The Large Intestine : The large intestine has three parts, Caccum, Colon
and Rectum. The walls of the large intestine absorb much of the water.
So it is not lost from the body. The semi–solids which remain are called
faeces. They are stored in large intestine and passed out at intervals through
Steps involved : Ingestion→ Digestion → Absorption → Assimilation
→ Egestion

The Respiratory System
● The Respiratory System takes in oxygen from the air and expels carbon
dioxide and water vapour.
● The mammalian respiratory system consists of nasal cavity, nasopharynx,
larynx, trachea, bronchi, bronchioles and alveoli in lungs.
● The lungs are enclosed in double walled sacs called Pleura.
●  During inspiration, the diaphragm and external intercostals muscles
contracts, due to which diaphragm moves down. i.e. flattens the volume of
thorax increases, which lead to fall in pressure inside.
●  Expiration is carried out passively by relaxation of diaphragm and
intercoastal muscles. The volume of thorax decreases and pressure inside
thorax and lungs increases to expel the air to outside.
The Circulatory System
● The Circulatory System is constituted of following important parts :



1. Arteries : These are large thick elastic walled blood vessels that carries
blood from the heart to the limbs and organs. All arteries except the
pulmonary artery carry oxygenated blood.
2. Veins : A blood vessel that conveys blood from the capillary network in
the tissues/organs to the heart. All veins except the pulmonary vein carry
deoxygenated blood. They all have valves to prevent back flow of blood.
3. Heart : The human heart is situated in the thorax between the lungs with
its apex resting on the diaphragm is responsible for double circulation. It
consists of four chamber viz two Atria (Auricles) and two Ventricles. The
Auricles receive blood from the veins. The ventricles pump blood into the
arteries. Human heart beats at the rate of about 72/min in the resting
condition i.e. a single beat completes in 0.8 sec.
The Excretory System
●  Metabolism of different chemical susbstances produces different waste
products in the body. The process of elimination of these metabolic waste
products from the body is called excretion.
● Important constituent organs of the excretory system are :
1. Skin
●  Human skin possess glands for secreting two fluids on its sufrace viz.
sweat from sweat glands and sebum from sebaceous gland.
● Sweat is an aqueous fluid containing mainly sodium chloride, lactic acid,
urea, amino acids and glucose.
● Sebum is wax–like secretion which eliminates some lipids, hydrocarbons
and fatty acids.
2. Kidney
● Kidneys are a pair of the urine forming organs.
● Nephrons are functional units of kidney.
The Nervous System
●  They form urine and drain it ultimately into the pelvis of kidney, from
where the ureter arises.
●  Ureters from both the kidneys finally open into urinary bladder which
stores urine temporarily.
●  Urethra arises from the neck of the bladder and conducts urine to the
exterior.
● The act of temporary storage then expulsion of it is called micturition.
● In a normal person daily output of urine is about 1-1.5 litre.



●  Each time a person expels urine he/she loses 420 ml of urine normal
capacity of urinary bladder.
3. Lungs
● Lungs regularly participate in the excretion of some volatile materials by
respiration.
●  The entire volume of carbon dioxide, produced in the body, and some
moisture are regularly excreted in the expelled air.
4. Liver
●  Liver is principal organ for the excretion of cholesterol, bile pigments
(bilirubin and biliverdin) produced from dead RBC and inactivated products
of steroid hormones, some vitamins and many drugs. It is also the site of
production of urea from proteins and ammonia (deamination).
The Nervous System
●  The Nervous System receives information about external and internal
changes, conducts such information between different parts of the body and
coordinates their activities in the light of those changes.
● The Nervous System consists of the Central Nervous System, Peripheral
Nervous System and Autonomic Nervous System.
The Central Nervous System
● The Central Nervous System consists of brain and spinal cord.
● The forebrain consists of cerebrum, it is the largest part of human brain.
● Brain and spinal cord are covered by three connective tissue membranes–
pia matter, archnoid matter and dura matter.
●  An extra–cellular fluid called Cerebrospinal fluid produced by cerebral
plexus occurs between pia and archnoid matter. It affords some protection to
CNS against mechanical injury and shock, it exchanges materials.
●  Hypothalamus contain higher nerve centres for osmoregulation,
temperature regulation, hunger, thirst and emotional functions. It secretes five
neurohormones which control secretions of pituitary hormones, It synthesises
the posterior pituitary hormones.
●  Cerebellum is second largest portion of brain located at base, under the
cerebrum. It helps in maintaining body balance and posture, when walking,
running or swimming etc.
● Cerebral hemisphere is divided into frontal, parietal, temporal and occipital
lobes each with its own association centres.
● The spinal cord is present in a series of 31 sections called vertebrae.



Each segment giving rise to pair of spinal nerve.
● From brain it arise as an extension of medulla that comes out of cavity in
skull called formen of magnum.
Peripheral Nervous System (PNS)
● PNS is composed of spinal nerves and cranial nerves.
●  Neurons and nerve fibres conduct nerve impulses from the CNS to
peripheral organ are called efferent neurons.
● Neurons and nerve fibres conduct nerve impluses from periphery to CNS
are called afferent neurons.
● Neurons and nerve fibres which cause movement of the muscles are called
motor neurons.
● There are 31 pairs of spinal nerves and 12 pairs of cranial nerves among
humans.
Autonomic Nervous System (ANS)
●  Nerves conveying impulses to glands, involuntary muscles and heart
muscles constitute ANS.
●  ANS is divided into two Sympathetic and Parasympathetic nervous
system.
●  Sympathetic nerves enhance the force and rate of heart beat, constrict
blood vessel, raise arterial blood pressure, dilate pupils, relax urinary bladder
to help body adjust to changes.
● Parasympathetic nerves, decrease both rate and force of heart beat, dilate
many blood vessel, lower the blood pressure, constrict the pupils, contract
the urinary bladder. i.e. brings body back to normal after the change.
● Tongue (Taste) : position of taste sensation over tongue sweet-top, sour-
sides, bitter-back.
The Skeletal System Axial Skeleton (80 bones) Skull

● In skeletal system where two bones meet is called joint. It can be movable
allowing movement of bones or immovable. The moveable joints are hinge
joints or ball and socket type depending on type of motion, they permit. In
movable joints, bones have synovial membrane with synovial fluid to
facilitate easy movement.
Types of skeleton : There are three types of skeleton called.
(i) Hydrostatic skeleton : In organisms showing hydrostatic skeleton, the
musles contract to change the shape of coelom produces movement due to
the presence of fluid inside fluid-filed cavity such as in earthworms, round
worms, etc.
(ii) Exoskeleton : It is the skeleton made of chitin and calcium carbonate
found outside the body such as in insects.
Therefore animals with exoskeleton have to shed off the skeleton from time
to time growth.
(iii) Endo skeleton : It is comprised of bones and cartilages found in higher
animals such as birds and mammal.
The Endocrine System
●  Endocrine glands are ductless glands which secrete hormones into the
blood. Hormones are informational molecules. They regulate functions of
other organs and tissues. Their actions are more widespread but slower than
nerve impulses in coordinating the activities of different organs and tissues.

BLOOD
Blood



Blood Cells Blood Platelets Plasma Fluid matrix Erythrocytes Leukocyte
Neutrophilis Eosinophils Granulocytes Agranulocytes Basophilis Monocytes
Lymphocytes
● Blood is a fluid connective tissue along with lymph.
● Erythrocytes (RBC) are most numerous of the formed element of blood.
Their most characteristic feature is presence of haemoglobin and absence of
nucleus.
● The total number of erythrocytes per microlitre of blood is known as total
count of RBC. It averages 5 and 4.5 million percc of blood in adult man and
adult women respectively.
● The total count would be low in anaemia.
● The abnormal rise in total count in RBC is called polycythemia.
● Erythrocyte participate in transporting oxygen from lungs to tissue.
● CO2 is carried in both plasma and RBC as bicarbonate ions of sodium.
● In foetus, erythrocytes are mainly formed in the liver and spleen. But from
birth onwards, erythrocytes are formed in red bone marrow while destroyed
in liver and spleen (grave yard of RBC).
● Erythrocyte has average life of 120 days.
● Iron (Fe-II) and proteins are essential raw material while B12
and folic acid stimulate synthesis and maturation.
● Leukocytes (WBC) are devoid of haemoglobin and are nucleated.
● Neutrophils and monocytes protect the body against microbes.
●  Lymphocytes secrete antibodies in the blood to destroy microbes and
neutralise their toxin.
● The number of leukocytes per micro litre of blood is called total count of
WBC. It is normally 5000 in humans. The ratio of RBC to WBC is 1000 :1.
● Blood Platelets (thrombocytes) are non nucleated, round or oval, biconvex
cell.
● Their normal life span is a week.
● When blood vessel is injured, platelets get clamped at the injured spot and
release certain chemical called Platelet Factor, which promote blood
coagulation.
●  The extra–cellular fluid is strawcoloured, slightly alkaline and aqueous
called plasma, which is 30%-35% of blood.
● Plasma contains 92% of water and three major classes of plasma proteins –
albumin, globulin and fibrinogen. It serves as source of protein for tissue
cells.



●  Albumin and globulin retain water in blood plasma. A fall in plasma
protein leads to filtering out of excessive volumes of water from blood to
tissue.
● This is why hands and feet get swollen with accumulated fluid in person
suffering from dietary deficiency of protein.
●  Plasma functions in transport, body immunity, prevention of blood loss,
retention of fluid in blood, maintenance of pH, uniform distribution of heat.
Functions of Blood
1. Transport
(a) Respiratory gases (b) Nutrients
(c) Waste products (d) Hormones, enzymes, minerals
2. Protective Function
(a) Production of antibodies (b) Phagocytosis
(c) Clotting mechanism
3. Regulatory Function
(a) Body temperature (b) pH balance
(c) Fluid balance
Blood Coagulation
● Coagulation is brought about by hydrolysis of soluble fibrinogen of blood
plasma to insoluble fibrin. This is catalysed by an enzyme called thrombin.
Fibrin precipitate as network of fibres.
● Thrombin occurs in blood as an inactive globulin called Prothrombin.
●  In case of injury, Coagulation promoting substances called
Thromboplastins are released, which help in formation of enzyme
Prothromkinase.
This enzyme hydrolyses prothrombin to thrombin. Ca2+ ions and vit.k are
essential for both the activation and action of thrombin.
Body injury Prothrombin Thromboplastin Thrombin Fibrinogen Fibrin
(Blood Clot) Ca2 + vit – k
● In some people timely blood clotting does not take place. They are known
to suffer from haemophilia.
Blood Groups
● K. Landsteiner in 1900 classified human beings in four groups on the basis
of the reactions of their blood A, B, AB and O.
●  Blood compatibility depends upon chemicals called agglutinogen or
antigens on the surface of the red cells and chemicals called agglutinin or
antibodies in the plasma.



● There are two types of antigen: A and B; and two types of antibody: anti-A
and anti-B.
●  Blood Transfusion. Anti-A plasma agglutinates A red cells, and anti-B
plasma agglutinates B red cells. So these combinations of plasma and red cell
are incompatible as far as blood transfusion is concerned. However a child’s
blood group can not directly be used for providing the maternity or faterinity
as a father or mother with blood group A & B can have blood group AB or O
type too.

RBC of feotus occur causing anaemia and death. These days to prevent this
Rhogan containing defective unit anti Rh antibody are injected in pregnant
mother.

REPRODUCTIVE SYSTEM
●  Humans like mammals show sexual dimorphism with male and female
reproductive organs present in different individuals.
●  In males the primary sex organs are a pair of testes found in muscular
pouch called scrotum that hangs outside human body because development
of sperms requires 2°-3°C less temperature than body temperature.
●  During excitation, the sperms passes into urethra where secretions from
seminal vesicle and prostate gland takes place.
●  The seminal vesicle added fructose rich secretion to provide energy for
swimming of sperms and the secretion from prostate gland is basic in nature
to neutralise acidity of urine before it could damage the sperms.
● The mixture of mucus along with secretions from glands and sperm that is
ejaculated is called semen.
●  Female Reproductive System : In human females, a pair of ovaries
located in lower abdominal cavity on either side of vertebral column are the
primary female sex organ.
● In females ovary although primary oocytes are present right from birth as
their formation occurs during foetal stage, the development starts with
beginning of adolescence at an age of about 12 years when the first
menstrual cycle called menarche occurs to end with menstrual discharge on
28 days of the cycle.
●  In females these menstrual cycles continues upto age of 50 years when
menopause occurs and after which a mother cannot become pregnant.
● During this fertile period from menarche to menopause, female undergo
menstrual cycle and period when pregnant and a few months after child birth
(lactation amenorrhea) mother does not undergo menstrual cycle.
● During the course of a menstrual cycle the changes a female undergoes in
its reproductive system are regulated by sex hormone called oestrogen and
progesterone.
● The secretion of progesterone also called pregnancy hormone starts after
14th day of menstrual cycle when ovulation takes place in which an ovum is



released to be pushed into fallopian tube (oviduct) where act of fertilization
takes place.
● If successful fertilization takes place a zygote is formed and the embryo
development start to occur and complete in uterus where embryo gets
implanted and if the fertilization does not takes place, the dead ovum is
expelled from body in menstrual flow marking the end of menstrual cycle
and start the new one.
●  The developing embryo in uterus for its growth takes oxygen and
nourishment from a muscular connection called placenta. It also secrete the
hormone relaxin and HCG whose presence in urine is taken as pregnancy test
by a doctor.
● After the completion of pregnancy period also called gestation period of
270 days i.e. 9 months.
● The hormone oxytocin also called birth hormone from posterior pituitary
gland marks the beginning of parturition i.e. child birth.
● The first milk produced and secreted by mothers for breast feeding of her
child is called colostrum.
●  In a child number of changes takes place soon after birth in first three
months such as ossification of bones i.e. at birth total number of bones in an
infant are about 270 but in 3-months they are reduced to 206, the
development of RBC which takes place in liver of foetus now shifts to bone
marrow while the liver become the site for death of RBC therefore in most
cases an infant suffers from anemia after child birth. As you have learnt in
human health and diseases.
● An infant is ought to be vaccinated for number of diseases like T.B., Polio,
Diptheria, Tetanus and chicken pox etc.
GENETICS
●  The term ‘Genetics’ was coined by Watson to describe the study of
inheritance and variation and the factors controlling them.
●  Today the subject has three main subdivisions — Mendelian genetics,
population genetics and biochemical genetics.
● Heredity may be defined as the transmission of characteristics from one
generation to successive generations of living things.
● The first quantitative study of inheritance was carried out by Gregor John
Mendel on Garden Peas (Pisum sativum).
● Gene is a unit of heredity.



● The various forms of a gene are called alleles.
● Individual organisms in which the members of a pair of alleles are different
are said to be heterozygous.
● Individuals in which the members of a pair of alleles are same are said to
be homozygous.
● The Genotype is the genetic constitution of an organism.
● The observable characteristics of an organism constitutes its Phenotype.
● The ability of a gene to have many effects is called Pleiotropy.
● Pedigree analysis is a system to analyse the distribution and movement of
traits in the family tree.
● The Chromosome Theory of Inheritance by Sutton and Boveri states that
Mendelian genes are located on chromosomes, and it is the chromosomes
that segregate and assort independently.
●  The sexually reproducing animals have, in each of their cells, a set of
chromosomes called autosomes and a pair of sex chromosomes (X and Y).
● In human females there are two X chromosomes and in males one X and
one Y Chromosome.
● Har Gobind Khorana, along with his associates, manufactured short DNA
molecules having a known sequence of bases (Gene).
● DNA molecules synthesize messenger RNA molecules also of known base
sequence. These RNA molecules then direct protein synthesis and form the
sequence of the proteins, the codes were directly established. This is a
milestone in the history of genetics. For this, Khorana shared with M.W.
Nirenberg and R.H. Holley (1968) Nobel prize in medicine.
● Central Dogma, DNA Transcription mRNA Translation Protein
Genetic Disorders
● Albinism : caused by recessive gene, which block the conversion of amino
acid tyrosine to melanin. Effect is absence of pigment in skin, hair and iris of
eyes.
● Down syndrome (Mongolism) : caused by trisomy of chromosome 21.
Effect is physically and mentally retarded patient with round face and widely
separated eyes.
●  Glactosemia : caused by recessive autosomal gene, results in defect in
enzyme system utilizing sugar glactose lead to high–level of galactose in
blood; resulted in development of cataract and brain damage.



●  Hemophilia : caused by sex–linked recessive gene; produce defective
protein, essential for clotting of blood.
●  Huntington’s disease : caused by dominant autosomal gene; lead to
progressive deterioration of nervous system. Peculiar feature developed
between 30 to 40 years.
● Klinefelter syndrome : because of additional X-chromosome; resulted in
improper development of testes.
● Muscular Dystrophy : caused by sex linked recessive gene, resulted in
childhood muscle weakness suffering and atrophy.
●  Phenylketonuria (PKU) : caused by recessive autosomal gene in
homozygous condition, results into accumulation of acid phenylanine and
brain damage. It can be prevented by giving phenylalanine diet.
●  Sickle Cell Anaemia : caused by recessive gene that lead to abnormal
haemoglobin molecule. RBC are sickle shaped with reduced haemoglobin.
●  Tay-Sach disease : caused by autosomal recessive gene-resulted in
cerebral degeneration, epilepsy, paralysis and blindness.
● Thalassemia : (Cooley’s anaemia) mostly in children caused by recessive
gene-resulted abnormality in haemoglobin structures.
● Turner Syndrome : caused by lack of X chromosome (OX); resulted in
phenotypical female but have rudimentary sex organ and mammary gland.
FOOD AND NUTRITION
Balanced Diets
● A diet is balanced if it contains all the nutrients in the correct amount.
● A balanced diet is related to the state of one’s age, health and occupation.
● Carbohydrates, proteins, fats, vitamins and minerals are nutrients.
● Carbohydrates and fats : provide energy.
● Proteins : growth and repair or replacement of cellular mechanism.
● Vitamin and minerals : aid vital reaction in cells and tissues.
●  Roughage : adds bulk in food for proper digestion otherwise leads to
constipation.
●  For Pregnant woman and children protein requirement is high for body
building.
● For Hard–working adult, carbohydrates and fats requirement is high.
Food Tests
● Starch : With iodine solution, brown colour it gives blue black colour.



●  Reducing sugar : Sugars such as glucose gives Benedict’s solution or
Fehling's solution test.
●  Benedict’s solution blue in colour gives yellow to brick red colour
depending on percentage of the glucose present in given solution.
● For oligosaccharide like galactose or maltose. The sugar is first acidified
and heated to break into simple sugar before testing it for presence of simple
sugar.
● Proteins : The proteins present are tested by Biurette test. In the test the
given amount is mixed with equal volume of ammonium or sodium
hydroxide and then heated with solution of copper sulphate.
● The appearance of purple colour shows the presence of protein.
● Fats : These are tested by rubbing them on a piece of paper to which it
makes oily and translucent/transparent. It is also tested by adding in solution
of ethanol with which oils gives cloudy white precipitate.
Important Facts
● Carbohydrates are more suitable for the production of energy in the body
than protein and fats because carbohydrate molecules contain relatively more
oxygen than the others.
● Carbohydrates are supplied to the tissues mainly as blood sugar.
● Carbohydrates are also stored in the tissues as glycogen.
●  Athelets, labourers doing heavy work and mountaineers should live on
high carbohydrates.
● Yield of 1 gm carbohydrate is 17 KJ or 4.1 K cal.
● Carbohydrates account for 60 – 80% energy requirement.
● Fats gives slightly more than twice of energy than carbohydrates because it
contains less oxygen.
● 1 gm fat or oil give 37 kJ or 9.45 k Cal.
● Butter, ghee, cheese, milk, egg, yolk, nut, meat are rich sources of fat.
● Fat is insoluble in water and soluble in organic solvent.
● Fats are stored as lipid in our body.
● Few fats that cannot be synthesized by our body are called essential fat.
The most important of these is Linoleic acid (all vegetable oil contain it,
except coconut oil).
● Linoleic, linolenic and arachidonic acids are essential fatty acids.
● Unsaturated fat : Low melting point, more reactive.
● Saturated fat : Occur mostly in animal fat, high melting point, solidify at
room temperature.



● The principal nutritional role of proteins is to build tissue structures.
● Proteins are made up of 20 amino acids. Some of the amino acids cannot
be synthesized in the animal body and must be supplied with food in
adequate amounts.
●  They are called Essential Amino Acid. Other Amino Acids may be
synthesized in the body, are called Non-Essential Amino Acid.
● Eight–amino–acids are considered essential for human nutrition.

ECOLOGY
ECOLOGY
●  Ecology is a scientific study of the reciprocal relationship between
organisms (including microbes, plants, animals, man) with their
environment. It is defined ‘as a scientific study of the relationship of the
living organisms with each other and with their environment’.
● The term Ecology was coined by famous biologist Ernest Haickel.
● The term ecology has been derived from two Greek words namely Oikos
meaning home and
Logos meaning study.
● Literally it is the study of the home of nature.
● Ecosystems are parts of nature where living organisms interacts amongst
themselves and with their physical environment.
● The term ecosystem was coined by Sir Arthur Tansley.
ECOSYSTEM
● Ecosystems consist of life forms existing in a symbiotic relationship with
their environment.
● Two main components exist in an ecosystem : (a) Abiotic (b) Biotic
(a) Abiotic component : The abiotic components of any ecosystem are the
non-living properties of the environment.
●  The abiotic components consist of the non-organic aspects of the
environment that determine what life forms can thrive.
(b) Biotic Component : The biotic components of ecosystem are the life
forms that inhabit it.
●  The biotic components include living organisms comprising plants,
animals and microbes and are classified according to their functional
attributes into Producer and Consumers.
Primary Producers : Autotrophs
● Primary producers are basically green plants and certain bacteria and algae.
● They synthesise carbohydrates from simple inorganic raw materials like
CO2 and water in the presence of sunlight by the process of photosynthesis
for themselves.
●  In terrestrial ecosystem, producers are basically herbaceous and woody
plants.



● In aquatic ecosystem producers are various species of microscopic algae.
Consumers : Heterotrophs or Phagotrophs
● Consumers cannot produce their own food.
● They depend on organic food derived from plants, animals or both.
Ecotone
● Ecotone is a zone of junction between two or more diverse ecosystems
Examples – Mangrove, grassland, estuary and river bank.
Niche
● A niche is the unique Functional role or place of a species in an ecosystem.
It is a description of all the biological, physical and chemical factors that a
species needs to survive. It is a unique for species as it plays an important role
in conservation of organism.
● No two species have exact identical niches.
Biome
●  The terrestrial part of the biosphere is divisible into enormous regions
called biomes, which are characterised by climate, vegetation, animal life
and general soil type.
● The boundaries of biome is determined by the climate
● No two biomes are alike.
● The most important climatic factors are temperature and precipitation.
● Example-Biome Tundra, Taiga, Tropical rain forest, Temperate deciduous
forest, Savanah, Desert etc.
FOOD-CHAINS
● The sequence of eaten and being eaten, produces transfer of food energy
and it is known as food chain.
● A food chain starts with producer and ends with top carnivores.
● The shorter the food chain, the greater is the available energy.
●  Small herbivores consume the vegetable matter and convert them into
animal matter. These herbivores are eaten by large carnivores.
FOOD WEB
● A food web illustrates, all possible transfers of energy and nutrients among
the organisms in an ecosystem.
● The food web provides more than one alternative for food to most of the
organisms in an ecosystems.
POLLUTANTS AND TROPHIC LEVEL



Pollutants especially nondegradablo ones move through the various trophic
levels in an ecosystem.
Movement of these pollutants involves two main processes :
(i) Bioaccumulation
(ii) Biomagnification.
Bioaccum ulation It refers to how pollutants enter a food chain.
● In bioaccumulation there is an increase in concentration of a pollutant from
the environment to the first organism in a food chain.
Biomagnification
● Biomagnification refers to the tendency of pollutants to concentrate as they
move from one trophic level to the next.
●  Thus in biomagnification there is an increase in concentration of a
pollutant from one link in a food chain to another
Biomagnification
In order for biomagnification to occur, the pollutant must be: longlived,
mobile, soluble in fate, biologically active.
● If a pollutant is short-lived, it will be broken down before it can become
dangerous. If it is not mobile, it will stay in one place and is unlikely to be
taken up by organisms. If the pollutant is soluble in water, it will be excreted
by the organism.
Pollutants that dissolve in fats, however, may be retained tor a long lime.
BIOTIC INTERACTION
● Organisms living in this earth are interlinked to each other in one way or
other. The interaction between the organisms is fundamental for its survival
and functioning of ecosystem as a whole.
Biotic Interaction
(+) Benefited for both species (–) harmed
(0) Neither Benefited nor harmed.
1. Mutualism (+) (+)
2. Commensalism (+) (0)
3. Competition (–) (–)
4. Predation (+) (–)
5. Parasitism (+) (–)
6. Amensalism (–) (0)
7. Neutralism (0) (0)
Nutrient Cycling :



The nutrient cycle is a concept that decribes how nutrients move from the
physical environment to the living organisms, and subsequently recycled
back to the physical environment.
● This movement of nutrients from the environment into plants and animals
and again back to the environment is essential for life and it is the vital
function of the ecology of any region.
Water Cycle
●  The water cycle is the continuous circulation of water in the
earthatmosphere system.
● This water cycle is driven by solar energy.
● This water is stored in many reservoirs like — atmosphere, oceans, lakes,
rivers, soils, glaciers, snowfield and ground water.
● Water move from one reservoir to another by the processes of evaporation,
transpiration, condensation, precipitation, deposition etc.
The Carbon Cycle
● Carbon is found in the atmosphere in the form of CO2.
●  In the presence of sunlight, plants take up CO2 from the atmosphere
through their leaves.
● The plants combine CO2 with water which is absorbed by their roots from
the soil.
●  In the presence of sunlight plants are able to form carbohydrates that
contain carbon. This process is called photosynthesis.
● Plant releases O2 into the atmosphere on which animals depend for their
respiration.
● Plants therefore help in regulating and monitoring the percentage of O2 and
CO2 in the earth's atmosphere.
● Both plants and animal release CO2 during respiration.
● When the plants and animal die, they return their Carbon to the soil. In this
way Carbon cycle completes.
Nitrogen Cycle
● Our atmosphere contains 78% free nitrogen (N2).
● All animals including aquatic animals need nitrogen to produce proteins
necessary for life.
● Nitrogen is an essential constituent of protein and is a basic building block
of all living tissue.
● Animals can use N2 in free form.
● They must consume N2 by eating N2 compounds.



● There are four process in the N2
cycle —
1. Nitrogen Fixation 2. Digestion 3. Decomposition 4. Waste production
1. Nitrogen Fixation
● The N2 cycle shows how the free N2 in the air is turned into N2 compounds
and put into the soil by nitrogen fixing bacteria. This process is called
Nitrogen Fixation.
● Nitrogen fixing bacteria's are Azotobacter, Clostridium Rhizobium as well
as blue green algae (e.g. Analsaena, spirulina)
● Plants that soak up the nitrogen compounds in the soil are called legumes,
oats, peas, beans, corn etc
Atmospheric N2 Fixation
●  Thunder storms and lightning are the common photochemical and
electrochemical reactors in nature which convert N2 to oxides of nitrogen.
● They get dissolved in water forming nitrous acid and nitric acid which is
turn combine with other salts to produce nitrates.
SUCCESSION
Succession is a universal process of directional change in vegetation, on an
ecological time scale.
● Succession occurs when a series of communities replace one another due
to large scale destruction either natural or manmade. This process continues
one community replacing another community, until a stable, mature
community develops.
Primary Succession
● In primary succession on a terrestrial site the new site is first colonized by
a few hardy pioneer species that are often microbes, lichens and mosses.
The pioneers over a few generations alter the habitat conditions by their
growth and development.
Secondary Succession
● Secondary succession occurs when plants recognize an area in which the
climax community has been disturbed.
● Secondary succession is the sequential development of biotic communities
after the complete or partial destruction of the existing community.
BIODIVERSITY
●  Biodiversity is defined as the variability among living organism for all
sources, including terrestrial, marrine and other aquatic ecosystems and the



ecological complexes of which they are part, this includes diversity within
species, between species and of ecosystem.
●  India is a recognised as one of the mega diverse countries, rich in
biodiversity and associated traditional knowledge.
● India accounts for nearly 7% of the recorded species.
● A healthy biodiversity provides a number of natural services, resources and
social benefits for a everyone.
TYPES OF BIODIVERSITY
1. Genetic Biodiversity
● Genetic diversity refers to genetic variation within species.
2. Species Diversity
● Species diversity refers to the numbers and kinds of living organisms.
● Species differ from one another, markedly in their genetic make up, don't
interbreed in nature.
3. Ecological Diversity
● Ecological diversity denotes the variety of habitats, biological communities
and ecolgical process.
Causes for the loss of biodiversity : There are number of the causes that
have combined together to adversely affect the existence of the present
biodiversity. Some of these causes include:
Fragmentation : The fragmentation of the natural areas has led to the habitat
loss. Among all, the animals which require large territories like birds and
mammals are the most affected along with migratory animals.
Over-exploitation : The excessive exploitation of a species for human need
has led to the extinct of several species such as of Dodo bird.
Invasion of alien species : The invasion of an alien species whether
voluntarily or involuntarily always threatens the existence of the native
species such as
● Introduction of Water Hyacinth (Echhornia crassipes) now called Terror of
Bengal to check water pollution is clogging the water ways leading to the
death of aquatic plants and animals.
● Introduction of Nile perch, predator fish in lake Victoria in South Africa
has led to the extinction of the native Cichlid fish.
Monoculture: it refers to the mode of agriculture or forestry where only a
specific of economical value is reared and managed for commercial gains.



This however led to the extinction of the other species in spite of their
biological importance in maintain the ecological balance.
BIODIVERSITY HOT SPOTS
● A biodiversity hotspot is a biogeographic region that is both a significant
reservoir of biodiversity and is threatened with destruction.
● Most of these hotsopots are located in the tropics.
Criteria for declaring region as
biodiversity hotspot
1. Region must contain at leost 0.5% or 1,500 species of vascular plants as
endemic.
2. It has to have lost atleast 70% of its primary vegetation.
Indian Hotspots
Major — 1. The Himalayan Hot spot.
2. Indo-Burma Hotspot
3. Western Ghats Hotspot Minor — Sundaland Hotspot (in this Hotspot
Nicobar Island included)
HOT SPOT OF THE WORLD
●  A biodiversity hotspot is a biogeographic region with a significant
reservoir of biodiversity that is under threat from humans.
●  The 25 biodiversity hotspot as indicated is Norman Myers ‘Biodiversity
hotspot for conservation priorities’.
● 9 hotspot have since been added thus there are 34 hotspot in the world.
BIOSPHERE RESERVE
● Biosphere reserves are areas of terrestrial and coastal ecosystem promoting
solutions to reconcile the conservation of biodiversity with its sustainable
use.
● They are internationally recognized, nominated by national governments
and remain under soverign jurisdiction of the state where they are located.
● Biosphere Reserve protect larger areas of natural habitat and often include
one or more National Parks.
●  Protection is granted not only to the Flora and Fauna of the protected
region, but also to the human communities who inhabits in these reign.
● The biosphere reserve programme was launched by the UNESCO in 1971
under the aegis of its ‘man and biosphere programme’.



●  Department of Environment is nodal agency for biosphere reserve
programs.
● The BSR is declared by a notification by the central government of India
and the state government.
● The central government assumes the responsibility of meeting the costs of
setup while the state government would setup derived machinery.
● Nilgiri the first biosphere reserve in India came into being in 1986.
● There are 18 BSRs in India till today.
Biosphere Reserves of India
1. Manas — 1984 — Assam
2. Dibru Saikhowa — 1997 — Assam

16. Great Nicobar — 1989 — Andman Nicobar Islands
17. Cold desert — 2009 — Himachal Pradesh
18. Nanda Devi — 1988 — Uttarakhand
Man and Biosphere Reserve (MAB)
● MAB was launched in 1971.
●  UNESCO’s man and the biosphere programme (MAB) is an inter
governmental scientific programme that aims to establish a scientific basis
for the improvement of relationship between people and their environment.
● Participating countries establish MAB programme is defined by its main
governing body, the International Coordinating Council.
● The MAB secretariat is based at UNESCO’s Division of Ecological and
Earth Science, at UNESCO’s head quarter is in Paris.
●  Out of 18 biosphere reserve 10 are a part of the World Network of
Biosphere Reserve based on the UNESCO Man and the Biosphere



Programme list.

WILDLIFE PROTECTION
PROJECT TIGER
● Project Tiger is a tiger conservation programme launched in 1973 by the
government of India during PM Indira Gandhi's tenure.
● The main objective of this programme is to ensure a viable population of
tiger for economic, scientific, cultural, aesthetic and ecological values.
● Census of tiger began in 2006.
National Green Tribunal (NGT)
The NGT has been established on 18.10.2010 under the National Green
Tribunal Act, 2010, for effective and expeditious disposal of cases relating to
environmental protection and conservation of forests and other natural
resources including enforcement of any legal right relating to environment
and giving relief and compensation for damages to persons and property and
for matters connected therewith or incidental thereto.
Organisation :
● The sanctioned strongth of the tribunal is currently 10 expert members and
10 judicial members although the act allows for up to 20 of each.

● Total forest Cover as percentage of geographical area is 21.34%.
● State with largest total forest cover is Madhya Pradesh.
● State having highest forest cover as percentage of its area is Mizoram.



● North-East States covers around 25% of total forest cover of the country.

MANGROVE FOREST
● Mangroves are trees and bushes growing below the high water level of
spring tides which exhibits remarkable capacity of salt water tolerance.
●  Mangroves are characteristic littoral plant formation of tropical and
subtropical sheltered coostlines.
● The best locations are where abundant silt is brought down by rivers. It
produce phenatophores to over come respiration problem in the anaerobic
soil conditions.
CORAL REEFS
● Coral is actually a living animal.
● Coral has a symbiotic relationship.
● The builder of coral reefs are tiny animals called Polyps.
● When polyps die, they leave their limestone skeltons behind.
● The limestone is colonised by new polyps. Therefore, a coral reef is build
up of layers of these skeletons covered ultimately by living polyps.
● Coral reefs are a habitat of various sea animals.
● Coral reefs are called a rain forest of ocean.
● They are 0.2% of total area of oceans.
●  They are generally found between line of cancer and line of capricorn
(23½ North — 23½ South)
● Year 2008 was declared as an International coral reef year.



● Environment and Forest Ministry set up a National Coral Reef Research
Center in Port Blair.
● Four Major Coral Reefs in India
1. Gulf of Kachchh
2. Gulf of Mannar
3. Lakshadweep Island
4. Andman and Nicobar Islands
● Among the four major reefs areas of India, Andman and Nicobar Islands
are found to be-very rich in species diversity.
Threat to Coral Reefs
● 75% of the world’s coral reefs are in danger due to local and global causes.
● Local causes — Intensive fishing
● Global causes—
● Increasing in temperature due to global warming

● Pollution

● Sediment loading

● Cyclone etc.
Importance of Coral reefs
● Coral reefs are natural protective barriers against erosion and storm surge.
● They are the reproductive centre of many species.
● They provide substrate for mangroves.
Coral Bleaching
● Bleaching or the paling of coral colour occurs when 1.the densities of zoo
xanthellae decline or 2.the concentration of photosynthetic pigments within
the zooxanthellae fall.
GREEN HOUSE
● A Green House is a building made of glass chambers in which plants are
grown in cold countries or in cold elevated areas.
● There is a continuous increase in temperature in green house even when
the outside temperature remained low. It protects plants from frost.
THE GREEN HOUSE EFFECT
Greenhouse effect is term for the role the atmosphere plays in helping to
warm the Earth’s surface.



● The atmosphere is largely transparent to incoming solar radiation, because
it is largely comprised ofroys of shorter wave lengths. Much of this
oncoming radiation is absorbed by the Earth’s surface. The hot surface of the
earth than reemits heat energy at long-wave infrared rays.
While some of the intrared radiation passes through the atmosphere, a large
part of it is absorbed and redistributed back by goses such as carbon dioxide,
Methane, nitrous oxide, halocarbons, water vapour and ozone in the
atmosphere. This heating effect is called the green house effect and it causes
global warming. The green house effect has two types :
GREEN HOUSE GASES
Carbon Dioxide
● Carbon Dioxide (CO2)
● Methane (CH4)
● Nitrous oxide (N2O)
● Sulphur hexafluoride (SF6)
● Chlorofluorocarbons (CFC)
● Primary green house gas.
● Hydrofluorocarbons (HFC5)
● Perflurocarbons (PFC5)
● Ozone (O3)
● Water Vapour (H2O)
Rankwise Top 10 Green House Emitting Countries : Rankwise
1. China 2. USA 3. European Union 4. India 5. Russia 6. Indonesia 7. Brazil
8. Japan 9. Canada 10. Mexico
ACIDIFICATION
● Acid rain is the rainfall that has been acidified. It is formed when oxides of
sulfur and nitrogen react with the moisture in the atmosphere.
● Rain considered as acid rain when PH goes below 5.6.
Compounds Causing Acid rain Compounds Sources
Sulphur Soil and oceans, volcanic eruptions, Biological process in the soil,
Burning of coal, petroleum products, Industries.
Nitrogen Lightening, volcanic eruption, Biological activity, Industries
Formic Acid Biomass burning.
Other Acids Chlorine, Phosphoric acid, Hydrochloric acid, carbon monoxide,
Carbon dioxide Ocean acidification



CO2 effect on Ocean Acidification
Less Plankton Production Less Productive Fisheries Coral reaf can’t grow
Impact of Acid Rain :
●  The exchange between hydrogen ions and the nutrient cations like
Potassium and magnesium in the soil cause leaching of the nutrients, making
the soil infertile.
● This is accompanied by a decrease in the respiration of soil organisms.
●  An increase in ammonia in the soil due to a decrease in other nutrients
decrease the rate of decompositions.
● Surface erosion in building stones, black crust formation etc. Fading colour
change in monuments.
Ocean Acidification :
● Oceans are an import reservoir for CO2.
● They absorb one third of CO2
produced by anthropogenic activities.
● Ocean acidification is the change of ocean chemistry.
●  When the uptake of atmospheric CO2 by the ocean increases, the
concentration of H2 ions in the ocean increases, but when the concentration
of CO2 ions decreases, the pH of the oceans decreases and the ocean become
less alkaline this process is called ocean acidification.
Ozone layer and its protection
● Ozone is a natural gas. It is found in atomosphere.
●  Ozone consists three atoms of oxygen bound together in non linear
fashion.
● The chemical symbol is O3.
● About 90% of the ozone in the atmosphere is contained in the stratosphere
(range 10-50km) above Earth’s surface, the highest concentrations bteween
about 20 and 40 km. It is very important because it absorbs ultraviolet rays
which is harmful for human being, animals & plants.
● The ultraviolet rays cause direct damage to the genetic material, on DNA
of animals and plants cells.
● Dobson is the unit of Ozone Layer measurement.
Sources of Ozone Depletions
●  Chlorofluorocarbons (CFC)–The most widely used Ozone depleting
substance, accounting for over 80% of total stratozpheric ozone depletion.
● Used as coolonts in refrigerators, freezers and air conditioners in buildings
and cars.



●  Also found in industrial solvents, dry-cleaning agents and hospital
sterilants.
● Methyl Chloroform–used mainly in industries for vapour decreasing, cold
cleaning, aerosols, adhesives and chemical processing.
Efforts taken for the Conservation of Ozone layer Vienna Convention
● The Vienna conference is 1985 the first internation conference on ozone
layer depletion.
● Convention in this conference entered into force in 1988.
● The convention does not include legally binding reduction goals for the use
of CFCs and other Ozone Depleting Substances.
● These legally binding reduction goals for Ozone Depleting Substances are
laid out in the Montreal protocol.
Montreal Protocol It is an international treaty to protect the ozone layer by
phasing out the Ozone Depleting Substances across the world.
●  The toealy was put forward in 1987, this treaty entered into force on
January 1989.
National Air Quality Index (AQI)
● For monitoring the air quality in major cities across the country on the real
time basis government launched Air Quality Index is April, 2015. It is colour
based index and shows the quality of air by different colour.
●  There are six AQI categories, namely Good, Satisfactory, Moderately
polluted, poor, very poor and severe.

###COMPUTER HISTORY###
● The genesis of mechanical/digital computing can be traced back to Blaise
Pascal (1623-1662) and GW Liebnitz (1646-1716). However, Charles
Babbage (1792-1871) was the first to imagine a machine that could process
data. He designed first difference engine and analytical engine and an all
purpose calculating machine. He is rightly known as the father of computer
science.
Computer “Computer is an electronic device for performing arithmetic
and logical operations”, or “Computer is a device or flexible machine to
process data and convert it into information. Computer is programmable
meaning that it all depends upon what program computer is using for
performing a particular function.
● Programme : It is a set of instructions fed into the computer on what to
do.
●  Hardware : It is a actual machine that does whatever the Software
(computer programs) tells it to do.
Four basic operations of a computer
1. Input : This is for the purpose of inserting or feeding data into the
computer by means of an input device like keyboard.
2. Processing : Some kind of processing is done in the computer to take out
or transform the data in some way.
3. Output : The computer produces output on a device, such as printer,
scanner or a monitor, that shows the result of processing operations.
4. Storage : The computer stores the result of processing operations for
future use in some storage device like hard disk, compact disk drive (CD



Rom) or a floppy disk.
Characteristices of Computer
● Accuracy ● Versatility ● Memory ● Diligence ● Speed ● Storage ● Reliability
● Automated
Components of a digital computer
A digital computer can be broadly classified as a collection of four
components.
They are :1. Input unit 2. Output unit 3. Central Processing Unit 4. Memory
(auxiliary)
Input Unit : The Input Unit provides an interface between the users and the
machine, for inputing data and instruction etc.
Some common input devices are listed below :
1. Keyboard 2. Mouse 3. Voice data entry 4. Joystick 5. Light pen 6. Scanner
7. Secondary storage devices 8. Punch Card Readers 9. Optical Mouse 10.
Speech Recognisition system
Output Unit : The Output Unit also provides an interface between the user
and the machine. The output unit receives the data from the CPU in the form
of binary bits.
This is then converted into a desired form (graphical, audio, visual etc.)
understandable by the user.
Some common output devices are :
1. Visual Display Unit (Monitor)
2. Printers
3. Speakers
4. Secondary Storage Devices
5. Tape Reader
6. Plotter The input and output unit are collectively referred to as
‘peripherals’
Central Processing Unit (CPU) :
It is the brain of the computer system. The input and output devices may vary
for different application, but there is only one CPU for a particular computer.
The central processing unit can be further divided into :
1. The Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU)
2. The Control Unit
3. Main Memory
Functions of CPU :
1. Performs arithmetic calculations such as addition, subtraction etc.



2. Performs logical decisions.
3. Performs data transmission.
4. It can manipulate tasks such as word processing.
Arithmetic Logic Unit (ALU) :
The arithmetic logic unit performs all arithmetic and logic calculations on the
data it receives. Arithmetic Calculations like addition, subtraction,
multiplication division, exponentiation etc. Logical Calculations are basically
decision making statements e.g. A > B, It decides whether A is greater than
B or not. If A is greater, the statement is true and logical '1' would be
generated otherwise illogical '0' would be generated.
Control Unit : The Control unit controls the entire operations of the
computer and the CPU. It controls all the other devices connected to the
CPU, i.e. Input devices, Output devices, Auxiliary Memory etc.
Hence, the control unit acts as the nerve centre of the computer.
Main Memory Unit : The main memory also known as the primary
memory. It is a part of the central processing unit and is a combination of
both RAM (random access memory) and ROM (read only memory).
RAM : The random access memory is read write memory i.e. information
can be read as well as written into this type of memory. It is volatile in
nature, i.e., the information it contains is lost as soon as the system is shut
down unless ‘saved’ for further usage by users.
It is basically used to store programs and data during the computer’s
operation.
ROM : It contains information that can only be read. It is nonvolatile or
permanent in nature. It is basically used to store permanent programs such as
program for the functioning of the monitor.
Cache Memory : The cache memory lies in the path between the processor
and the main memory.
